WDVV solutions associated with the genus one holomorphic differential
Chaabane REJEB

Abstract
Consider the Hurwitz space ℋ 1 ​ ( n 0 , … , n m ) \mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m}) of genus one ramified coverings of fixed degree with m + 1 m+1 prescribed poles of order n 0 + 1 , … , n m + 1 n_{0}+1,\dots,n_{m}+1 , respectively. Based on a recent formula proved in [ 35 ] , we derive an explicit solution to the WDVV equations associated with the Dubrovin-Frobenius manifold structure on ℋ 1 ​ ( n 0 , … , n m ) \mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m}) induced by the normalized holomorphic differential ϕ \phi . The resulting solution is a function of 2 + 2 ​ m + ∑ j = 0 m n j 2+2m+\sum_{j=0}^{m}n_{j} variables and is written in terms of Bell polynomials, Eisenstein series as well as Weierstrass functions. In addition, by means of flat coordinates related to ϕ \phi , we construct Landau–Ginzburg superpotentials for these Frobenius manifold structures. MSC (2020) primary: 53D45, 35C05; secondary: 35G20, 58J60. Keywords: WDVV equations, Frobenius manifolds, holomorphic differential, Bell polynomials, Eisenstein series, Weierstrass functions.



1
Introduction

In this paper, our focus lies on providing explicit examples of genus one solutions to the Witten-Dijkgraaf-E.Verlinde-H.Verlinde (WDVV) associativity equations
[
16
,
45
]
:

F_{\alpha}F_{1}^{-1}F_{\beta}=F_{\beta}F_{1}^{-1}F_{\alpha},\quad\quad\alpha,\beta=1,\dots,N,

(1.1)

where
F=F(t^{1},\dots,t^{N})
and
F_{\alpha}
denotes the Hessian matrix of
\partial_{t^{\alpha}}F
for each
\alpha
with the requirement that the matrix
F_{1}
is constant and nondegenerate. The Geometric framework behind the WDVV equations has been developed by Dubrovin, culminating in the remarkable theory of Frobenius manifolds
[
18
]
.

A Hurwitz space
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{H}_{g}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
of dimension
N
in genus
g
is the moduli space of branched coverings of genus
g
of the Riemann sphere
\mathbb{P}^{1}
with
N
finite simple branch points and a prescribed ramification profile over the point at infinity of fixed branching degrees
n_{0}+1,\dots,n_{m}+1
. The
N
simple branch points can be used as natural local parameters on the complex manifold
\mathcal{M}
. In
[
18
]
, Dubrovin constructed a family of
N
semi-simple Frobenius manifold structures on
\mathcal{M}
, each of them is induced by a specific choice of particular differential
\omega_{0}
(called primary) defined on the underlying Riemann surfaces. The prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
of the obtained Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold determined by
\omega_{0}
arises as an example of quasi-homogeneous solutions to the WDVV equations (
1.1
). Nevertheless, calculating the explicit form of WDVV solutions
\big\{\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}\big\}
presents a notably intricate task.

This work aims to provide an answer to this question in the special case of the Hurwitz space
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
in genus one and where the primary differential
\omega_{0}=\phi
is the normalized holomorphic differential, but without any restriction on the combinatorial parameters
(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
. The expression for the obtained WDVV prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\phi}
associated with the holomorphic primary differential
\phi
is stated in Theorem
4.1
below.

Before moving on to the ideas of our approach, let us review the known results in this direction. The first explicit example of a genus one prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\phi}
was obtained by Dubrovin
[
18
]
in the particular case where the Hurwitz space is that of the Weierstrass elliptic curves. Moreover, Bertola realized the
\phi
-Frobenius manifold structure on the Hurwitz space
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n)
as that on the orbit space of Jacobi group of type
A_{n}
and computed for
n=2,3,4
the corresponding WDVV solution
[
13
,
14
]
. Other explicit examples are found in
[
15
]
and arose from the four dimensional Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold structure and its deformation on the space
\mathcal{H}_{1}(0,0)
. The method employed to calculate the mentioned examples uses a Dubrovin bilinear pairing formula written in terms of objects defined on the underlying Riemann surfaces. We refer to Lecture 5 in
[
18
]
(and
[
40
]
) for details.

Recently, the author established in
[
35
]
a new alternative formula to calculate the WDVV prepotential of any Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold of dimension
N
in genus
g
. The structure of this formula is based on a precise duality relation between flat coordinates and on the degrees of the Dubrovin primary differentials. The concept of the degree of a primary differential
\omega_{0}
arises in a natural way as the power of
\lambda
(where
\lambda
defines a branched covering of the Riemann sphere) in the integral representation formula of
\omega_{0}
with respect to the canonical symmetric bidifferential
W(P,Q)
. Here we would like to point out that, as it was observed in
[
40
]
(see also
[
35
]
), the symmetric bidifferential
W(P,Q)
offers a new viewpoint to redescribe the ingredients of the Frobenius manifold structure on the Hurwitz space
\mathcal{M}
in genus
g
.
The Hurwitz-Frobenius manifolds in genus one and their ingredients, including the genus one bidifferential
W(P,Q)
, primary differentials and the corresponding prepotentials
\big\{\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}\big\}
are presented in the next section. The new method for calculating the WDVV solutions
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
is given by formula (
2.56
) below.

The first key element of our approach to obtaining the explicit expression for the prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\phi}
is to rewrite the new formula (
2.56
) in the special case of the holomorphic differential which is of degree
0
. This leads to formula (
4.5
) below which shows that only few terms are unknown and (most of them) occur as the first coefficients of the Laurent expansions of some meromorphic differentials near the poles of a genus one branched covering.

The calculation of these terms using flat coordinates associated with the holomorphic differential
\phi
requires several technical steps and their expressions involve Weierstrass functions, Bell polynomials and Eisenstein series. In particular, we construct Landau-Ginzburg (LG) superpotentials of the Hurwitz space
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
.

This paper is organized as follows. In the next section we collect the properties of the main ingredients used in this work. This includes reviews on Bell polynomials, Weierstrass functions and the Dubrovin-Frobenius manifold structures on the considered Hurwitz space
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
. Section 3 is technical, it is devoted to calculating the explicit form of the coefficients of the Laurent expansions of Abelian differentials of the second and third kind whose poles are among those of a fixed covering belonging to the Hurwitz space
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
.
Section 4 focuses on the main result of this work and some applications. The last section deals with the WDVV solutions associated with the deformed Frobenius-Hurwitz manifold determined by the so-called holomorphic
q
-differential, with
q
being a fixed complex parameter.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Bell polynomials

This subsection deals with some properties of partial ordinary Bell polynomials that will be repeatedly used in this paper. The reader is referred to
[
6
,
9
,
11
]
for details.

Let
n\geq k\geq 0
be two integers. The partial ordinary Bell polynomial
\mathcal{B}_{n,k}
is defined by
[
6
,
9
,
11
]

\mathcal{B}_{n,k}:=\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-k+1}):=k!\sum_{\bigtriangleup(n,k)}\bigg(\prod_{\mu=1}^{n-k+1}\frac{x_{\mu}^{j_{\mu}}}{j_{\mu}!}\bigg),

(2.1)

where the summation is over the set
\bigtriangleup(n,k)
of all partitions of
n
into
k
parts, that is, over all nonnegative integers
j_{1},\dots,j_{n-k+1}
satisfying

\begin{split}&j_{1}+\dots+j_{n-k+1}=k;\\
&j_{1}+2j_{2}+\dots+(n-k+1)j_{n-k+1}=n.\end{split}

The partial ordinary Bell polynomials have the generating function:

\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}x_{j}t^{j}\bigg)^{k}=\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-k+1})t^{n}.

(2.2)

In particular, we have the following special cases of partial ordinary Bell polynomials:

\forall\ n\geq 1,\quad\mathcal{B}_{0,0}=1;\quad\quad\mathcal{B}_{n,0}=0;\quad\quad\mathcal{B}_{n,1}=x_{n};\quad\quad\mathcal{B}_{n,n}=x_{1}^{n};\quad\quad\mathcal{B}_{n,2}=\sum_{j=1}^{n-1}x_{j}x_{n-j}.

(2.3)

Moreover, (
2.2
) implies that partial ordinary Bell polynomials enjoy the following recursion relation:

\mathcal{B}_{n+k,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-k+1})=\sum_{j=0}^{\min(n,k)}\binom{k}{j}x_{1}^{k-j}\mathcal{B}_{n,j}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n-j+2}).

(2.4)

The partial ordinary Bell polynomials are closely related to the (better-known) partial exponential Bell polynomials
\mathbf{B}_{n,k}
:

\mathbf{B}_{n,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-k+1})=\frac{n!}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{n,k}\Big(\frac{x_{1}}{1!},\dots,\frac{x_{n-k+1}}{(n-k+1)!}\Big).

(2.5)

The nth (exponential) complete Bell polynomial is defined by
\mathbf{B}_{0}=1
and

\mathbf{B}_{n}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbf{B}_{n,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-k+1})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{n!}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{n,k}\Big(\frac{x_{1}}{1!},\dots,\frac{x_{n-k+1}}{(n-k+1)!}\Big).

(2.6)

The partial ordinary (and exponential) Bell polynomials
\mathcal{B}_{n,k}
(
\mathbf{B}_{n,k}
) are homogeneous of degree
k
:

\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(cx_{1},\dots,cx_{n-k+1})=c^{k}\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-k+1}),\quad\quad\forall\ c.

(2.7)

On the other hand, according to formulas (11.11) and (11.12) in
[
9
]
(see also
[
11
]
(Chapter 3)), it is known that the partial exponential Bell polynomials satisfy the recurrence relations:

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{n+1,k+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-k+1})=\sum_{j=k}^{n}\binom{n}{j}x_{n+1-j}\mathbf{B}_{j,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{j-k+1});

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}_{n+1,k+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-k+1})=\frac{1}{k+1}\sum_{j=k}^{n}\binom{n+1}{j}x_{n+1-j}\mathbf{B}_{j,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{j-k+1}).

By (
2.5
), we easily see that the analogues of these formulas for the partial ordinary Bell polynomials take the following form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{n+1,k+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-k+1})=\frac{k+1}{n+1}\sum_{j=k}^{n}(n+1-j)x_{n+1-j}\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{j-k+1});

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{n+1,k+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-k+1})=\sum_{j=k}^{n}x_{n+1-j}\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{j-k+1}).

(2.9)

In particular,

\mathcal{B}_{n+1,k+1}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n-k+1})=\frac{k+1}{n+2+k}\sum_{j=k}^{n}(n+2-j)x_{n+1-j}\mathcal{B}_{j,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{j-k+1}).

(2.10)

In order to make formulas simpler during the upcoming sections, we introduce the following rational function involving partial ordinary Bell polynomials.

Definition 2.1

Let
\mu,k
be two positive integers satisfying
\mu-k+1\neq 0
. Let
\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}:=\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{\mu+1})
be the rational function defined by

\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}:=\frac{1}{(\mu-k+1)}\sum_{n=0}^{\mu}\left\{\Big((\mu+1-n)x_{\mu+1-n}\Big)\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\binom{-k}{\ell}x_{1}^{-k-\ell}\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}(x_{2},\dots,x_{n-\ell+2})\right\},

(2.11)

where
\displaystyle\binom{-k}{\ell}:=(-1)^{\ell}\binom{k+\ell-1}{\ell}
is the generalized binomial coefficient.

We have the following remarks:

1.

The function
\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}
is polynomial in the variables
x_{2},\dots,x_{\mu+1}
.

2.

Because of the homogeneity property (
2.7
) of partial Bell polynomials, we observe that the function
\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}
satisfies:

\forall\ c\neq 0,\quad\quad\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}(cx_{1},\dots,cx_{\mu+1})=c^{1-k}\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{\mu+1}).

(2.12)

Lemma 2.1

Let
\mu,k
be two positive integers satisfying
\mu-k+1\neq 0
. Then the multivariate rational function (
2.11
) is also given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}&=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\mu-1}\frac{1}{\ell+1}\binom{-k}{\ell}x_{1}^{-k-\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\ell+1}(x_{2},\dots,x_{\mu-\ell+1}).\end{split}

(2.13)

Proof:
When
\mu=1
, then we easily check that (
2.13
) is valid. Assume that
\mu\geq 2
. To simplify, let us introduce the following notation

\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}:=\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n-\ell+1}),\quad\quad\text{with}\quad y_{j}=x_{j+1}.

Then from expression (
2.11
) for
\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}
, we can write

\displaystyle(\mu-k+1)x_{1}^{k}\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}

\displaystyle=(\mu+1)y_{\mu}+\sum_{n=1}^{\mu}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\binom{-k}{\ell}(\mu+1-n)y_{\mu-n}y_{0}^{-\ell}\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}(y_{1},\dots,y_{n-\ell+1})

\displaystyle=(\mu+1)y_{\mu}+y_{0}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\mu}\binom{-k}{\ell}y_{0}^{-\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\ell}+\sum_{n=1}^{\mu-1}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\binom{-k}{\ell}(\mu+1-n)y_{\mu-n}y_{0}^{-\ell}\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}

\displaystyle=(\mu+1)\mathcal{B}_{\mu,1}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{\mu-1}\binom{-k}{\ell+1}y_{0}^{-\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\ell+1}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\mu-1}\binom{-k}{\ell}y_{0}^{-\ell}\bigg(\sum_{n=\ell}^{\mu-1}(\mu+1-n)y_{\mu-n}\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}\bigg)

\displaystyle=(\mu+1-k)\mathcal{B}_{\mu,1}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\mu-1}\binom{-k}{\ell+1}y_{0}^{-\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\ell+1}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\mu-1}\binom{-k}{\ell}\frac{\mu+\ell+1}{\ell+1}y_{0}^{-\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\ell+1}

\displaystyle=(\mu+1-k)\mathcal{B}_{\mu,1}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{\mu-1}\binom{-k}{\ell}\frac{k+\ell}{\ell+1}y_{0}^{-\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\ell+1}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\mu-1}\binom{-k}{\ell}\frac{\mu+\ell+1}{\ell+1}y_{0}^{-\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\ell+1}

\displaystyle=(\mu+1-k)\sum_{\ell=0}^{\mu-1}\binom{-k}{\ell}\frac{\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\ell+1}}{(\ell+1)y_{0}^{\ell}},

where we used the fact that
\mathcal{B}_{\mu,1}(y_{1},\dots,y_{\mu})=y_{\mu}
in the third equality and applied recurrence relation (
2.10
) in the forth equality.

\square

2.2
Weierstrass functions and Eisenstein series

In this subsection, basic properties of the Weierstrass functions are presented. There exist several texts on the subject of elliptic functions and related topics. For instance, we refer to the monographs
[
3
,
5
,
8
,
12
,
21
,
26
,
42
]
.

Let
\tau
be a complex number with strictly positive imaginary part and
\mathbb{L}
be the lattice
\mathbb{L}:=\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}
generated by
1
and
\tau
. The non-normalized Eisenstein series are defined on the upper half-plane by

G_{2k}=G_{2k}(\tau)=\sum_{w\in\mathbb{L},w\neq 0}w^{-2k},\quad\quad\quad k\geq 2,

(2.14)

and
G_{2k+1}\equiv 0
. It is known that
G_{2k}
is a modular form of weight
2k
, which means that

G_{2k}\Big(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\Big)=(c\tau+d)^{2k}G_{2k}(\tau),\quad\quad\quad\forall\ \begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z}).

(2.15)

Expression (
2.14
) can be extended to
k=1
in the following way:

G_{2}=G_{2}(\tau)=2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{2}}+\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z},n\neq 0}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{1}{(m+n\tau)^{2}},

(2.16)

but then the series is not absolutely convergent. The Eisenstein series
G_{2}
is a quasi-modular form of weight 2
[
47
]
, since it satisfies

G_{2}\Big(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\Big)=(c\tau+d)^{2}G_{2}(\tau)-{2{\rm i}\pi}c(c\tau+d).

(2.17)

It is known that for each integer
k\geq 1
, the Eisenstein series
G_{2k}
is a periodic function of period
1
and has the following Fourier expansion (see for instance
[
5
]
(Section 3.10) and
[
8
]
(Chapter VI)):

G_{2k}(\tau)=2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{2k}}+2\frac{(2{\rm i}\pi)^{2k}}{(2k-1)!}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{2k-1}(n)e^{2{\rm}in\pi\tau},

(2.18)

where
\sigma_{k}(n)
is the sum of the k-th powers of all the positive divisors of
n
.

The
\mathbb{L}
-Weierstrass functions
\wp
,
\zeta
and
\sigma
are respectively defined by:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\wp(v)=\wp(v|\tau):=\frac{1}{v^{2}}+\sum_{w\in\mathbb{L},w\neq 0}\Big(\frac{1}{(v-w)^{2}}-\frac{1}{w^{2}}\Big);\end{split}

(2.19)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\zeta(v)=\zeta(v|\tau):=\frac{1}{v}+\sum_{w\in\mathbb{L},w\neq 0}\Big(\frac{1}{v-w}+\frac{1}{w}+\frac{v}{w^{2}}\Big);\\
\end{split}

(2.20)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sigma(v)=\sigma(v|\tau):=v\prod_{w\in\mathbb{L},w\neq 0}\big(1-\frac{v}{w}\big)\exp\Big(\frac{v}{w}+\frac{v^{2}}{2w^{2}}\Big).\end{split}

(2.21)

The function
\wp
is elliptic (doubly periodic) with respect to the lattice
\mathbb{L}
and the functions
\zeta
and
\sigma
are quasi-elliptic since they satisfy (
2.28
) below.

Here is the list of properties of the three functions that will be important in what follows.

1.

Differential equations for the
\wp
-function:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\wp^{\prime 2}(v)=4\wp^{3}(v)-g_{2}\wp(v)-g_{3};\end{split}

(2.22)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\wp^{\prime\prime}(v)=6\wp^{2}(v)-\frac{g_{2}}{2};\\
&\wp^{\prime\prime\prime}(v)=12\wp^{\prime}(v)\wp(v);\\
&\wp^{(4)}(v)=120\wp^{3}(v)-18g_{2}\wp(v)-12g_{3},\end{split}

(2.23)

where
g_{2}
and
g_{3}
are the Weierstrass invariants given by the Eisenstein series
G_{4}
and
G_{6}
:

g_{2}=g(\tau):=60G_{4},\quad\quad\quad g_{3}=g_{3}(\tau)=140G_{6}.

(2.24)

2.

The Weierstrass function
\zeta
satisfies:

\zeta^{\prime}(v):=-\wp(v).

(2.25)

3.

The function
\zeta
is the logarithmic derivative of
\sigma
:

\sigma^{\prime}(v)/\sigma(v)=\zeta(v).

(2.26)

4.

We have the following modular properties:

\begin{split}&\sigma\Big(\frac{v}{c\tau+d}\Big|\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\Big)=(c\tau+d)^{-1}\sigma(v|\tau);\\
&\zeta\Big(\frac{v}{c\tau+d}\Big|\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\Big)=(c\tau+d)\zeta(v|\tau);\\
&\wp\Big(\frac{v}{c\tau+d}\Big|\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\Big)=(c\tau+d)^{2}\wp(v|\tau),\end{split}\quad\quad\quad\forall\ \begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z}).

(2.27)

5.

The functions
\zeta
and
\sigma
satisfy the following quasi-periodicity properties:

\begin{split}&\zeta(v+2\omega_{j})=\zeta(v)+2\zeta(\omega_{j});\\
&\sigma(v+2\omega_{j})=-\sigma(v)e^{2\zeta(\omega_{j})v+2\omega_{j}\zeta(\omega_{j})},\end{split}\quad\quad\quad j=1,2,3,

(2.28)

where
\omega_{1}=1/2
,
\omega_{2}=\tau/2
and
\omega_{3}=\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}
are the half periods.

6.

The periods
\omega_{1}=1/2
,
\omega_{2}=\tau/2
and the quasi-periods of
\zeta
are related by the Legendre relation:

2\omega_{2}\zeta(\omega_{1})-2\omega_{1}\zeta(\omega_{2})=\tau\zeta(1/2)-\zeta(\tau/2)={\rm{i}}\pi.

(2.29)

7.

Addition theorems for the functions
\wp
and
\zeta
:

\displaystyle\wp(u+v)=-\wp(u)-\wp(v)+\frac{1}{4}\bigg(\frac{\wp^{\prime}(u)-\wp^{\prime}(v)}{\wp(u)-\wp(v)}\bigg)^{2};

(2.30)

\displaystyle\zeta(u+v)=\zeta(u)+\zeta(v)+\frac{\wp^{\prime}(u)-\wp^{\prime}(v)}{2\big(\wp(u)-\wp(v)\big)}.

(2.31)

8.

The Laurent expansion of the Weierstrass functions
\wp
and
\zeta
near the origin are given respectively by (see
[
5
]
(Theorem 1.11) and
[
8
]
):

\displaystyle\wp(v)=\frac{1}{v^{2}}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}(2\ell+1)G_{2\ell+2}v^{2\ell}=\frac{1}{v^{2}}+\sum_{\ell=2}^{\infty}(\ell+1)G_{\ell+2}v^{\ell};

(2.32)

\displaystyle\zeta(v)=\frac{1}{v}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}G_{2\ell+2}v^{2\ell+1}=\frac{1}{v}-\sum_{\ell=3}^{\infty}G_{\ell+1}v^{\ell},

(2.33)

where
G_{2\ell+1}\equiv 0
and
G_{2\ell}
,
\ell\geq 2
, are the non-normalized Eisenstein series (
2.14
).
Note that equalities (
2.32
) and (
2.33
) are valid whenever
v
satisfies

0<|v|<\rho:=\min\big\{|w|:\quad w\in\mathbb{L},w\neq 0\big\}.

9.

The Jacobi (odd) function
\theta_{1}
is defined by

\theta_{1}(v)=\theta_{1}(v|\tau):=-i\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{n}e^{{\rm{i}}\pi\tau(n+1/2)^{2}+2{\rm{i}}\pi(n+1/2)v}.

(2.34)

Weierstrass functions are related to the Jacobi
\theta_{1}
-function by (see
[
12
]
, Section 13.20, and
[
3
,
42
]
):

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sigma(v|\tau)=e^{\zeta(1/2|\tau)v^{2}}\frac{\theta_{1}(v|\tau)}{\theta^{\prime}_{1}(0|\tau)};\end{split}

(2.35)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\zeta(v|\tau)=\frac{\sigma^{\prime}(v|\tau)}{\sigma(v|\tau)}=2\zeta(1/2|\tau)v+\frac{\theta^{\prime}_{1}(v|\tau)}{\theta_{1}(v|\tau)};\end{split}

(2.36)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\wp(v|\tau)=-\zeta^{\prime}(v|\tau)=-2\zeta(1/2|\tau)-\frac{\theta^{\prime\prime}_{1}(v|\tau)}{\theta_{1}(v|\tau)}+\bigg(\frac{\theta^{\prime}_{1}(v|\tau)}{\theta_{1}(v|\tau)}\bigg)^{2}.\end{split}

(2.37)

2.3
Frobenius manifold structures on Hurwitz spaces in genus one

2.3.1
Frobenius manifolds

Definition 2.2

A
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
(\mathcal{A},\circ,e)
supplied with a nondegenerate and symmetric inner product
\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}:\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}
is a Frobenius algebra if

i)

the algebra
(\mathcal{A},\circ,e)
is associative, commutative with unity
e
;

ii)

the multiplication
``\circ"
is compatible with
\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}
, in the sense that

\mathop{\langle x\circ y,z\rangle}=\mathop{\langle x,y\circ z\rangle},\quad\quad\forall\ x,y,z\in\mathcal{A}.

A Frobenius algebra
(\mathcal{A},\circ,e,\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle})
is called semi-simple if it contains no nilpotent element.

Definition 2.3

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a complex manifold of dimension N. A semi-simple Frobenius manifold structure on
\mathcal{M}
is a data of
\big(\mathcal{M},\circ,e,\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle},E\big)
such that each tangent space
T_{t}\mathcal{M}
is a semi-simple Frobenius algebra varying analytically over
\mathcal{M}
with the additional properties:

F1)

the metric
\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}_{t}
on
\mathcal{M}
is flat (but not necessarily real and positive);

F2)

the unit vector field
e
is flat with respect to the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
of the flat metric
\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}
, i.e.
\nabla e=0
;

F3)

the tensor
\big(\nabla_{v}\mathbf{c}\big)(x,y,z)
is symmetric in four vector fields
v,x,y,z\in T_{t}\mathcal{M}
, where
\mathbf{c}
is the symmetric 3-tensor

\mathbf{c}(x,y,z):=\mathop{\langle x\circ y,z\rangle},\quad\forall\ x,y,z\in T_{t}\mathcal{M};

F4)

the vector field
E
is covariantly linear, i.e.
\nabla\nabla E=0
, and

\displaystyle\begin{split}&[E,e]=-e,\\
&[E,x\circ y]-[E,x]\circ y-x\circ[E,y]=x\circ y,\\
&\big(Lie_{E}\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}\big)(x,y):=E\mathop{\langle x,y\rangle}-\mathop{\langle[E,x],y\rangle}-\mathop{\langle x,[E,y]\rangle}=(2-D)\mathop{\langle x,y\rangle},\end{split}

(2.38)

for some constant
D
, called the charge of the Frobenius manifold. The vector field
E
is called Euler vector field.

From
[
18
]
, we know that each Frobenius manifold
\big(\mathcal{M},\circ,e,\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle},E\big)
gives rise to a quasi-homogeneous solution
F
to
the WDVV equations (1.1), called the prepotential of the Frobenius manifold. For completeness, let us briefly describe the construction of the WDVV prepotential
F
.
First, since the metric
\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}
is flat by
F1)
, it follows that locally there exist flat coordinates
\{t^{\alpha}:\ 1\leq\alpha\leq N\}
such that the matrix
(\eta_{\alpha\beta})=\big(\mathop{\langle\partial_{t^{\alpha}},\partial_{t^{\beta}}\rangle}\big)_{\alpha\beta}
is constant. Second, the symmetry property of the 4-tensor
\nabla\mathbf{c}
in the condition
F3)
implies that there is a function
F=F(t^{1},\dots,t^{N})
such that its third derivatives (referred to as the 3-point correlation functions in topological field theory) give the 3-tensor
\mathbf{c}
:

\partial_{t^{\alpha}}\partial_{t^{\beta}}\partial_{t^{\gamma}}F=\mathbf{c}\big(\partial_{t^{\alpha}},\partial_{t^{\beta}},\partial_{t^{\gamma}}\big).

(2.39)

In particular, the prepotential
F
is unique up to addition of quadratic polynomial functions of
t^{1},\dots,t^{N}
.
Third, due to the flatness property of the unit vector field
e
(i.e.
\nabla e=0
), by making a linear change of flat coordinates, the unit field
e
can be taken of the form
e=\partial_{t^{1}}
. As a consequence, the entries of the constant Gram matrix of the flat metric
\mathop{\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle}
satisfy

\eta_{\alpha\beta}:=\mathop{\langle\partial_{t^{\alpha}},\partial_{t^{\beta}}\rangle}=\partial_{t^{1}}\partial_{t^{\alpha}}\partial_{t^{\beta}}F.

Now observing that the Frobenius algebra multiplication
``\circ"
is given by

\partial_{t^{\alpha}}\circ\partial_{t^{\beta}}=\eta^{\gamma\varepsilon}\mathop{\langle\partial_{t^{\alpha}}\circ\partial_{t^{\beta}},\partial_{t^{\varepsilon}}\rangle}\partial_{t^{\gamma}}=\eta^{\gamma\varepsilon}\mathbf{c}\big(\partial_{t^{\alpha}},\partial_{t^{\beta}},\partial_{t^{\varepsilon}}\big)\partial_{t^{\gamma}},\quad\quad\quad\text{with}\quad(\eta^{\alpha\beta})=\big(\eta_{\alpha\beta}\big)^{-1},

(summation over the repeated indices is assumed) and using (
2.39
), we conclude that the associativity property of the law
``\circ"
is equivalent to the WDVV equations (
1.1
) for
F
.

Lastly, let us deal with the quasi-homogeneity of the function
F
which occurs as a consequence of the requirements in (
2.38
). Indeed, (
2.38
) yields that the Lie derivative, along the Euler vector field
E
, of the symmetric 3-tensor
\mathbf{c}
satisfies:

\begin{split}\big(Lie_{E}.\mathbf{c}\big)(x,y,z)&:=E.\mathbf{c}(x,y,z)-\mathbf{c}\big([E,x],y,z\big)-\mathbf{c}\big(x,[E,y],z\big)-\mathbf{c}\big(x,y,[E,z]\big)\\
&=(3-D)\mathbf{c}(x,y,z).\end{split}

(2.40)

Writing
E
in the form
E=\sum_{\epsilon}E^{\epsilon}(t)\partial_{t^{\epsilon}}
, with
t=(t^{1},\dots,t^{N})
and making use of the requirement
\nabla\nabla E=0
, we obtain
\partial_{t^{\alpha}}\partial_{t^{\beta}}E^{\epsilon}=0
, for all
\alpha,\beta,\epsilon
. From this fact and relations (
2.39
) and (
2.40
), we arrive at

\big(Lie_{E}.\mathbf{c}\big)\big(\partial_{t^{\alpha}},\partial_{t^{\beta}},\partial_{t^{\gamma}}\big)=\partial_{t^{\alpha}}\partial_{t^{\beta}}\partial_{t^{\gamma}}E.F=(3-D)\partial_{t^{\alpha}}\partial_{t^{\beta}}\partial_{t^{\gamma}}F.

Thus the prepotential
F
is a quasi-homogeneous function of degree
3-D
, up to an addition of quadratic polynomials in
t^{1},\dots,t^{N}
. Here we point out that according to Dubrovin
[
18
]
(Lecture 1), a generalized quasi-homogeneity property is considered in the context of Frobenius manifolds. More precisely, a function
f
is called quasi-homogeneous of degree
\nu_{f}
with respect to the Euler vector field
E=\sum_{\alpha}\big(d_{\alpha}t^{\alpha}+r_{\alpha}\big)\partial_{t^{\alpha}}
if

\textstyle E.f=\nu_{f}f+\sum_{\alpha,\beta}A_{\alpha\beta}t^{\alpha}t^{\beta}+\sum_{\alpha}B_{\alpha}t^{\alpha}+C.

(2.41)

2.3.2
Hurwitz spaces in genus one

Let
m
and
n_{0},\dots,n_{m}
be nonnegative integers, with
\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)\geq 2
. The Hurwitz space
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
in genus one is the moduli space of equivalence classes of branched coverings
(\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}},\lambda)
, where

1.

\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
is the complex torus corresponding to the lattice
\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}
, with
\tau
being a complex number such that
\Im\tau>0
;

2.

\lambda:\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a meromorphic function of degree
\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)
having
m+1
prescribed poles
\infty^{0},\dots,\infty^{m}\in\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
of order
n_{0}+1,\dots,n_{m}+1
, respectively. The meromorphic function
\lambda
can be identified with an
\mathbb{L}
-elliptic (doubly periodic) function on the complex plane;

3.

The
N
zeros
P_{1},\dots,P_{N}
of the differential
d\lambda(P)
are simple and their finite
\lambda
-images
\lambda_{1}:=\lambda(P_{1}),\dots,\lambda_{N}:=\lambda(P_{N})\in\mathbb{C}
are distinct. The points
P_{1},\dots,P_{N}
are known as the simple ramifications points of the covering
(\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}},\lambda)
and their
\lambda
-projections
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}\in\mathbb{C}
are called simple branch points.

Two pairs
(\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}},\lambda)
and
(\mathbb{C}/{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}},\widetilde{\lambda})
are called equivalent if there is a biholomorphic map

h:\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C}/{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}}
such that
\lambda=\widetilde{\lambda}\circ{h}
.

According to the Riemann-Hurwitz formula the number
N
of finite branch points is determined by means of the number
m+1
of poles of
\lambda
as well as their orders
n_{0}+1,\dots,n_{m}+1
. More precisely, we have

N=2+2m+\sum_{i=0}^{m}n_{i}.

Furthermore, from
[
10
,
29
,
27
]
, it is known that the already described Hurwitz space
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
is a connected complex manifold of dimension
N
and the simple and finite branch points of a branched covering from
\mathcal{M}
serve as a system of local coordinates on
\mathcal{M}
. They also used as a set of canonical coordinates in the construction of the semi-simple Frobenius manifold structures on
\mathcal{M}

[
18
]
.

2.3.3
Genus one bidifferential

The bidifferential on the complex torus
\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
is defined by

W(P,Q)=\Big(\wp(z_{P}-z_{Q}|\tau)+2\zeta(1/2|\tau)\Big)dz_{P}dz_{Q},

(2.42)

where
P=z_{P}+\mathbb{L},Q=z_{Q}+\mathbb{L}\in\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
,
z_{P},z_{Q}\in\mathbb{C}
and
\wp,\zeta
are the Weierstrass functions defined by (
2.19
)-(
2.20
).
According to the already listed properties of the functions
\wp
and
\zeta
, we observe that the bidifferential
W(P,Q)
satisfies the following properties:

i)

It is a symmetric with respect to
P
and
Q
:
W(P,Q)=W(Q,P)
.

ii)

It has a pole of second order on the diagonal with biresidue 1:

W(P,Q)\underset{P\sim Q}{=}\left(\big(z_{P}-z_{Q}\big)^{-2}+\mathcal{O}(1)\right)dz_{P}dz_{Q}.

(2.43)

This follows directly from the Laurent expansion (
2.32
) of the function
\wp
near
0
.

iii)

Using the quasi-periodicity properties (
2.28
) of the function
\zeta
as well as the Legendre identity (
2.29
), we can see that periods of the differential
W(P,\cdot)
, with
P
fixed, are given by:

\oint_{Q\in a}W(P,Q)=0\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\oint_{Q\in b}W(P,Q)={2{\rm i}\pi}dz_{P},

(2.44)

where
\{a,b\}
is a canonical homology basis on
\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
such that the cycles
a
and
b
are identified to be the segments
a=[x,x+1]
and
b=[x,x+\tau]
.

In the complex manifold
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
, the dependence of the bidifferential
W(P,Q)
on the branch points
\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j}
of a covering
(\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}},\lambda)
is specified by Rauch’s variational formulas
[
25
,
34
,
46
]
:

\partial_{\lambda_{j}}W(P,Q)=\frac{1}{2}W(P,P_{j})W(P_{j},Q).

(2.45)

Here
W(P,P_{j})
denotes the evaluation of the bidifferential
W(P,Q)
at
Q=P_{j}
with respect to the standard local parameter
\mathbf{x}_{j}(Q)=\sqrt{\lambda(Q)-\lambda_{j}}
near the ramification point
P_{j}
:

W(P,P_{j}):=\frac{W(P,Q)}{d\mathbf{x}_{j}(Q)}\Big|_{Q=P_{j}}.

2.3.4
Dubrovin’s primary differentials and the corresponding Frobenius manifold structures

Let
[(\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}},\lambda)]
be a point of the Hurwitz space
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
. The genus one primary differentials on the torus
\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
are conveniently expressed by means of the bidifferential
W(P,Q)
(
2.42
) as follows
[
18
,
40
]
:

\begin{split}&\textbf{1.}\quad\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{i,\alpha}}(P):=\frac{\sqrt{n_{i}+1}}{\alpha}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(Q)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}}W(P,Q),\quad\begin{array}[]{ll}&i=0,\dots,m,\\
&\alpha=1,\dots,n_{i};\end{array}\\
&\textbf{2.}\quad\phi_{\mathbf{v}^{i}}(P):=\displaystyle\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}\ }\lambda(Q)W(P,Q),\quad\quad\quad i=1,\dots,m;\\
&\textbf{3.}\quad\phi_{\mathbf{s}^{i}}(P):=\displaystyle\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{i}}W(P,Q),\quad\quad\quad\quad i=1,\dots,m;\\
&\textbf{4.}\quad\phi_{\mathbf{r}}(P):=\displaystyle\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b}W(P,Q)=dz_{P};\\
&\textbf{5.}\quad\phi_{\mathbf{u}}(P):=\displaystyle\oint_{a}\lambda(Q)W(P,Q).\end{split}

(2.46)

Here the points
\infty^{0},\dots,\infty^{m}\in\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
are the assigned
m+1
poles of the covering
\lambda
and
n_{0}+1,\dots,n_{m}+1
are their orders, respectively and
\{a,b\}
is a canonical homology basis on
\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
with
a=[x,x+1]
and
b=[x,x+\tau]
.

All the differentials listed in (
2.46
) share the following particular integral representation formula with respect to the bidifferential
W(P,Q)
:

\omega_{0}(P)=c_{0}\int_{\ell_{0}}\big(\lambda(Q)\big)^{d_{0}}W(P,Q),

(2.47)

where
c_{0}
is a nonzero complex number,
d_{0}\geq 0
and
\ell_{0}
is a contour on the surface
\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
assumed to satisfy the following two conditions:

i)

\ell_{0}
does not passe through any of the ramification points
P_{j}
of the covering
(\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}},\lambda)
;

ii)

the
\lambda
-projection
\lambda(\ell_{0})\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
of
\ell_{0}
is independent of the branch points
\{\lambda_{j}\}_{j}
, where by this we mean that the contour
\lambda(\ell_{0})
does not change under small variations of
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{N}
.

These conditions imply that derivation, with respect to
\lambda_{j}
, under the integral sign in (
2.47
) is permitted and due to the Rauch variational formulas (
2.45
), we have

\partial_{\lambda_{j}}\omega_{0}(P)=\frac{1}{2}\omega_{0}(P_{j})W(P,P_{j}),\quad\quad\text{with}\quad\quad\omega_{0}(P_{j}):=\frac{\omega_{0}(P)}{d\mathbf{x}_{j}(P)}\Big|_{P=P_{j}}.

Definition 2.4

[
35
]
A differential
\omega_{0}
of the form (
2.47
) is called quasi-homogeneous differential and
the corresponding nonnegative number
d_{0}
is called the quasi-homogeneous degree of
\omega_{0}
.

The degrees of the primary differentials (
2.46
) are given by

\begin{split}°\big(\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{i,\alpha}}\big)=\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1};\\
°\big(\phi_{\mathbf{v}^{i}}\big)=deg\big(\phi_{\mathbf{u}}\big)=1;\\
°\big(\phi_{\mathbf{s}^{i}}\big)=deg\big(\phi_{\mathbf{r}}\big)=0.\end{split}

(2.48)

\bullet

Darboux-Egoroff metrics and the corresponding flat coordinates:
To each differential
\omega_{0}
among the list (
2.46
), Dubrovin associated a Darboux-Egoroff metric (i.e. diagonal with diagonal terms generated by a potential)

\eta(\omega_{0}):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\big(\omega_{0}(P_{j})\big)^{2}(d\lambda_{j})^{2}=\sum_{j}\bigg(\underset{P_{j}}{{\rm res}}\frac{\omega_{0}(P)^{2}}{d\lambda(P)}\bigg)(d\lambda_{j})^{2}

(2.49)

which is defined on the open subset
\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{0}}
of the Hurwitz space
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
determined by the conditions

\omega_{0}(P_{j})\neq 0,\quad\quad\forall\ j.

(2.50)

The rotation coefficients of the metric
\eta(\omega_{0})
do not depend on the chosen primary differential
\omega_{0}
and are given by
[
40
]

\beta_{ij}=\frac{1}{2}W(P_{i},P_{j})=\frac{W(P,Q)}{d\mathbf{x}_{i}(P)d\mathbf{x}_{j}(P)},\quad\quad i\neq j,\quad\mathbf{x}_{j}=\sqrt{\lambda(P)-\lambda_{j}}.

Moreover, the following
N
functions

\begin{split}&\mathbf{t}^{i,\alpha}(\omega_{0}):=\frac{\sqrt{n_{i}+1}}{\alpha}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}}\omega_{0}(P),\quad\begin{array}[]{ll}&i=0,\dots,m,\\
&\alpha=1,\dots,n_{i};\end{array}\\
&\mathbf{v}^{i}(\omega_{0}):=\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}\ }\lambda(P)\omega_{0}(P),\quad\quad\quad\quad i=1,\dots,m;\\
&\mathbf{s}^{i}(\omega_{0}):=p.v.\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{i}}\omega_{0}(P),\quad\quad\quad\quad i=1,\dots,m;\\
&\mathbf{r}(\omega_{0}):=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b}\omega_{0}(P);\\
&\mathbf{u}(\omega_{0}):=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\omega_{0}(P)\end{split}

(2.51)

give a system of flat coordinates of the metric
\eta(\omega_{0})
and the nonzero entries of the constant Gram matrix of the metric
\eta(\omega_{0})
are as follows:

\begin{split}&\eta(\omega_{0})\big(\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{i,\alpha}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{j,\beta}(\omega_{0})}\big)=\delta_{ij}\delta_{\alpha+\beta,n_{j}+1};\\
&\eta(\omega_{0})\big(\partial_{\mathbf{v}^{i}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{\mathbf{s}^{j}(\omega_{0})}\big)=\delta_{ij};\\
&\eta(\omega_{0})\big(\partial_{\mathbf{r}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{\mathbf{u}(\omega_{0})}\big)=1.\end{split}

(2.52)

From
[
18
]
, it is known that the principal value in the flat function
\mathbf{s}^{i}
is defined by omitting the divergent part of the integral as a function of the local parameters
\mathbf{z}_{0}(P):=\lambda(P)^{-\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}}
,
\mathbf{z}_{i}(P):=\lambda(P)^{-\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}}
near the points
\infty^{0}
and
\infty^{i}
, respectively.

\bullet

Duality relations
: As it has been observed in
[
35
]
, from (
2.52
), it follows that all the rows of the constant matrix of the Darboux-Egoroff metric
\eta(\omega_{0})
contain exactly one nonzero entry which is 1, bringing to duality relations between flat coordinates (
2.51
)

\mathcal{S}(\omega_{0}):=\big\{\mathbf{t}^{i,\alpha}(\omega_{0}),\mathbf{v}^{i}(\omega_{0}),\mathbf{s}^{i}(\omega_{0}),\mathbf{r}(\omega_{0}),\mathbf{u}(\omega_{0})\big\}.

More precisely, two flat coordinates
\mathbf{t}^{A},\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{S}(\omega_{0})
are called dual to each other with respect to the metric
\eta(\omega_{0})
if

\eta(\omega_{0})\big(\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{A}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}(\omega_{0})}\big)=1.

(2.53)

Due to (
2.52
), the operations
\mathbf{t}^{i,\alpha},\mathbf{v}^{i},\mathbf{s}^{i},\mathbf{r},\mathbf{u}
and the corresponding primary differentials (
2.46
) are connected by:

\begin{split}&\mathbf{t}^{({i,\alpha})^{\prime}}(\omega_{0})=\mathbf{t}^{i,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(\omega_{0}),\quad\quad\quad\quad\mathbf{v}^{{i}^{\prime}}(\omega_{0})=\mathbf{s}^{i}(\omega_{0}),\quad\quad\ \quad\mathbf{r}^{\prime}(\omega_{0})=\mathbf{u}(\omega_{0});\\
&\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{({i,\alpha})^{\prime}}}(P)=\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{i,n_{i}+1-\alpha}}(P),\quad\quad\quad\quad\phi_{\mathbf{v}^{i^{\prime}}}(P)=\phi_{\mathbf{s}^{i}}(P),\quad\quad\quad\phi_{\mathbf{r}^{\prime}}(P)=\phi_{\mathbf{u}}(P).\end{split}

(2.54)

Moreover, relations (
2.48
) together with (
2.54
) show that the degrees of primary differentials (
2.46
) enjoy the following duality relation:

deg\big(\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A}}\big)+deg\big(\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}}\big)=1,\quad\quad\quad\text{for all A}.

(2.55)

\bullet

Prepotential:
The following formula, proved in
[
35
]
, offers a new method to calculate the WDVV prepotential of the semi-simple Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold structure on the open set
\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{0}}
(
2.50
) induced by the quasi-homogeneous differential
\omega_{0}
(
2.47
):

\begin{split}&\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}=\frac{1}{2(1+d_{0})}\sum_{A,B}\frac{\big((d_{0}+d_{A})\mathbf{t}^{A}(\omega_{0})+\rho_{\omega_{0},\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A}}}\big)\big((d_{0}+d_{B})\mathbf{t}^{B}(\omega_{0})+\rho_{\omega_{0},\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{B}}}\big)}{1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}}\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}(\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{B^{\prime}}})\\
&\quad+\frac{\log(-1)}{4}\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{m}\mathbf{v}^{i}(\omega_{0})\mathbf{v}^{j}(\omega_{0})-\frac{3}{4(1+d_{0})}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\bigg(\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\bigg)\mathbf{v}^{i}(\omega_{0})\mathbf{v}^{j}(\omega_{0}),\end{split}

(2.56)

where
d_{0},d_{A},d_{A^{\prime}}
are respectively the degrees of the quasi-homogeneous differentials
\omega_{0}
,
\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A}}
,
\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}}
and
\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}
is the dual coordinate of
t^{A}
(
2.53
)-(
2.54
), while
\rho_{\omega_{0},\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A}}}
is the constant determined by:

\rho_{\omega_{0},\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A}}}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\delta_{\omega_{0},\phi_{\mathbf{s}^{i}}}\delta_{\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A}},\phi_{\mathbf{s}^{j}}}\Big(\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\Big)=\rho_{\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A}},\omega_{0}}.

(2.57)

Because of the added quadratic terms in formula (
2.56
), the functions
\big\{\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}\big\}
share the Hessian matrix which is as follows

\begin{split}\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{A}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{B}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}&=\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{B^{\prime}}}\right)+\mathbf{t}^{B^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}}\right)\right)\\
&=\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}\left(\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{B^{\prime}}}\right)+\frac{\log(-1)}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\big(1-\delta_{ij}\big)\delta_{\mathbf{t}^{A},\mathbf{v}^{i}}\delta_{\mathbf{t}^{B},\mathbf{v}^{j}}.\end{split}

(2.58)

Lastly, let us point out that with respect to flat coordinates (
2.51
), the ingredients of the Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold associated with the differential
\omega_{0}
are as follows
[
18
,
40
,
35
]
(using notation of
[
35
]
):

i)

the unit vector field is
e=\partial_{\textbf{t}^{A_{0}^{\prime}}}
, whenever
\omega_{0}=\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A_{0}}}
;

ii)

the Euler vector field is given by

E=\sum_{A}\big((d_{0}+d_{A})\mathbf{t}^{A}(\omega_{0})+\rho_{\omega_{0},\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A}}}\big)\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{A}(\omega_{0})},

(2.59)

where
d_{0}=deg(\omega_{0}),d_{A}=deg(\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A}})
and
\rho_{\omega_{0},\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A}}}
is the constant (
2.57
);

iii)

the symmetric 3-tensor
\mathbf{c}_{\omega_{0}}
has the form:

\begin{split}\mathbf{c}_{\omega_{0}}\big(\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{A}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{B}(\omega_{0})},\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{C}(\omega_{0})}\big)&=\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{A}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{B}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{C}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}}(P_{j})\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{B^{\prime}}}(P_{j})\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{C^{\prime}}}(P_{j})}{\omega_{0}(P_{j})}.\end{split}

(2.60)

We also emphasize that the WDVV equations (
1.1
) for the function
\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}
(
2.56
) take the following form

\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0},\alpha{AC}}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0},\beta{BC^{\prime}}}=\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0},\beta{AC}}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0},\alpha{BC^{\prime}}},\quad\quad A,B,C,\alpha,\beta=1,\dots,N,

(2.61)

where
N=2+2m+\sum_{j=0}^{m}n_{j}
and

\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0},ABC}:=\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{A}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{B}(\omega_{0})}\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{C}(\omega_{0})}\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{0}}.

Equations (
2.61
) follows from the fact that the constant Gram matrix
\eta
of the metric
\eta(\omega_{0})
satisfies
\eta^{AB}=\delta_{B,A^{\prime}}
, by (
2.52
).

3
Abelian differentials and the corresponding Laurent series

Throughout this section
(\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}},\lambda)
denotes a branched covering of the Riemann sphere
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, with
\lambda
being a meromorphic function having
m+1
poles
\infty^{0},\dots,\infty^{m}\in\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
of order
n_{0}+1,\dots,n_{m}+1
, respectively.
This section delves into the explicit form of Abelian differentials of the second and third kind whose poles are among the points
\big\{\infty^{0},\dots,\infty^{m}\big\}
, along with their Laurent expansions near these points. The obtained results involve some variables related to the normalized holomorphic differential and will serve as a crucial tool within the next sections.

3.1
Normalized holomorphic differential

The normalized holomorphic differential on the complex torus
\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
is defined by

\phi(P)=\frac{1}{2{\rm i}\pi}\oint_{b}W(P,Q)=dz_{P}

(3.1)

and its periods are given by

\oint_{a}\phi=1,\quad\quad\quad\oint_{b}\phi=\tau.

For
j=0,\dots,m
, consider the (genus one) Abel map
\mathcal{A}(P;\infty^{j})
defined by

\mathcal{A}(P;\infty^{j})=\int_{\infty^{j}}^{P}\phi=z_{P}-\infty^{j}.

(3.2)

Then,
\mathcal{A}(P;\infty^{j})
vanishes at
P=\infty^{j}
and its Taylor expansion near the point
P=\infty^{j}
is of the following form:

\mathcal{A}(P;\infty^{j})\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}x_{r}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{r},\quad\quad\quad\quad\lambda(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{-n_{j}-1}.

(3.3)

The coefficients
x_{r}(j)
in (
3.3
) occur also in the Taylor expansion for the holomorphic differential
\phi
near the point
P=\infty^{j}
:

\phi(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}rx_{r}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{r-1}\bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{j}(P),\quad\quad\quad\quad\lambda(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{-n_{j}-1}.

(3.4)

This implies that

x_{r}(j)=\frac{1}{r}\underset{\infty^{j}}{\rm res}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{r}{n_{j}+1}}\phi(P),\quad\quad\quad\forall\ r\geq 1

(3.5)

and, since the genus one normalized holomorphic differential has no zero, we have

x_{1}(j)\neq 0,\quad\quad\forall\ j=0,\dots,m.

In addition, in view of (
3.5
) and the residue theorem applied to the differential
\lambda(P)\phi(P)=\lambda(P)dz_{P}
, we obtain

\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{n_{j}+1}(j)=\sum_{j=0}^{m}\underset{\infty^{j}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)\phi(P)=0.

(3.6)

We now introduce the following operators related to holomorphic differential
\phi
which are of particular importance in this paper.

Definition 3.1

Let
\alpha
be a positive integer and
\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}
be the partial ordinary Bell polynomials defined by (
2.1
)-(
2.2
).
For
j=0,1,\dots,m
, we define the
(j,\alpha)
-Bell operator associated with the holomorphic differential
\phi
by

\mathbf{L}^{\phi}_{j,\alpha}:=\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\frac{1}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{\alpha-k+1}(j)\big)\partial_{v}^{k},

(3.7)

where
\infty^{j}
is one of poles of the covering
\lambda
of order
n_{j}+1
, the coefficients
x_{k}(j)
are defined by (
3.5
) and
v
is a variables independent of
x_{r}(j)
.

Let us mention that the Bell operator
\mathbf{L}^{\phi}_{j,\alpha}
acts naturally on functions as follows:

\mathbf{L}^{\phi}_{j,\alpha}[f](v):=\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\frac{1}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{\alpha-k+1}(j)\big)f^{(k)}(v).

(3.8)

Moreover, using the homogeneity property (
2.7
) of partial exponential Bell polynomials, we see that the Bell operator (
3.7
) can also be expressed by means of partial and complete exponential Bell polynomials (
2.5
)-(
2.6
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}^{\phi}_{j,\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha!}\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\mathbf{B}_{\alpha,k}\Big(1!x_{1}(j),\dots,(\alpha-k+1)!x_{\alpha-k+1}(j)\Big)\partial_{v}^{k}=\frac{1}{\alpha!}\mathbf{B}_{\alpha}\Big(1!x_{1}(j)\partial_{v},\dots,\alpha!x_{\alpha}(j)\partial_{v}\Big).

For future use, we will take into account the following Laurent expansions near the prescribed poles
\infty^{0},\dots,\infty^{m}
of the covering
(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{L},\lambda)
.

Proposition 3.1

For
j=0,\dots,m
, let
\infty^{j}
be a pole of
\lambda
of order
n_{j}+1
and
\mathcal{A}(P;\infty^{j})
be the function defined by (
3.2
). Then for any positive integer
k
we have

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{A}(P;\infty^{j})\big)^{k}\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{n,k}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{n-k+1}(j)\big)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{n};

(3.9)

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{A}(P;\infty^{j})\big)^{-k}\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left\{\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\binom{-k}{\ell}\frac{1}{\big(x_{1}(j)\big)^{k+\ell}}\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}\big(x_{2}(j),\dots,x_{n-\ell+2}(j)\big)\right\}\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{n-k}.

(3.10)

Here
\mathbf{z}_{j}(P):=\lambda(P)^{-\frac{1}{n_{j}+1}}
is the local parameter induced by the covering
(\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}},\lambda)
,
\mathcal{B}_{n,k}
denotes the partial exponential Bell polynomials defined by (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) and
x_{r}(j)
are the coefficients in the Taylor expansion (
3.4
) of the normalized holomorphic differential
\phi(P)=dz_{P}
defined by (
3.5
).

Proof:
Formula (
3.9
) follows from (
3.3
) and generating function (
2.2
) for partial Bell polynomials. Using again Taylor’s expansion (
3.3
) for the Abel map and (
2.2
), we obtain

\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{A}(P;\infty^{j})\big)^{-k}\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\frac{1}{\big(x_{1}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)\big)^{k}}\bigg(1+\frac{1}{x_{1}(j)}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}x_{r+1}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{r}\bigg)^{-k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\big(x_{1}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)\big)^{k}}+\frac{1}{\big(x_{1}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)\big)^{k}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\left\{\binom{-k}{\ell}\frac{1}{\big(x_{1}(j)\big)^{\ell}}\Bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}x_{r+1}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{r}\bigg)^{\ell}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\big(x_{1}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)\big)^{k}}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left\{\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}\binom{-k}{\ell}\frac{1}{\big(x_{1}(j)\big)^{k+\ell}}\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}\big(x_{2}(j),\dots,x_{n-\ell+2}(j)\big)\right\}\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{n-k}.

Therefore, since
\mathcal{B}_{n,0}=\delta_{n,0}
we arrive at (
3.10
).

\square

Corollary 3.1

With the notation of the preceding proposition, the following two equalities hold true for any positive integers
\alpha,k
:

\frac{\alpha}{k+1}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k+1}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{\alpha-k}(j)\big)={\underset{\infty^{j}}{\rm res}}\Big(\lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}}\big(\mathcal{A}(P;\infty^{j})\big)^{k}\phi(P)\Big)

(3.11)

and

\alpha\mathcal{R}_{\alpha+k-1,k}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{\alpha+k}(j)\big)={\underset{\infty^{j}}{\rm res}}\Big(\lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}}\big(\mathcal{A}(P;\infty^{j})\big)^{-k}\phi(P)\Big),

(3.12)

where
\mathcal{R}_{\alpha+k-1,k}
is the rational function given by (
2.11
) and (
2.13
).

Proof:
Using the Laurent expansions (
3.4
) and (
3.9
) and applying recurrence relation (
2.8
) , we deduce that

\displaystyle{\underset{\infty^{j}}{\rm res}}\Big(\lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})^{k}\phi(P)\Big)

\displaystyle={\underset{0}{\rm res}}\left\{\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}rx_{r}(j)\mathcal{B}_{n,k}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{n-k+1}(j)\big)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{n+r-\alpha-1}d\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=k}^{\alpha-1}(\alpha-n)x_{\alpha-n}(j)\mathcal{B}_{n,k}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{n-k+1}(j)\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{k+1}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k+1}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{\alpha-k}(j)\big).

Similarly, by (
3.4
), (
3.10
) and (
2.11
), we obtain

\displaystyle{\underset{\infty^{j}}{\rm res}}\Big(\lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})^{-k}\phi(P)\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{\alpha+k-1}\left\{(\alpha+k-n)x_{\alpha+k-n}(j)\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\binom{-k}{\ell}\frac{1}{\big(x_{1}(j)\big)^{k+\ell}}\mathbf{B}_{n,\ell}\big(x_{2}(j),\dots,x_{n-\ell+2}(j)\big)\right\}

\displaystyle=\alpha\mathcal{R}_{\alpha+k-1,k}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{\alpha+k}(j)\big).

\square

At the end of this subsection let us emphasize that the genus one bidifferential (
2.42
) can be rewritten in terms of the normalized holomorphic differential (
3.1
) as follows:

W(P,Q)=\Big(\wp(z_{P}-z_{Q}|\tau)+2\zeta(1/2|\tau)\Big)\phi(P)\phi(Q).

(3.13)

This formula and expansions (
3.3
), (
3.4
), (
3.9
) and (
3.10
) serve as key ingredients for deriving explicit forms for Abelian differentials of the second and third kind, as well as for their Laurent series expansions near the prescribed poles
\infty^{0},\dots,\infty^{m}
of the covering
(\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}},\lambda)
. This will be discussed in the upcoming two subsections.

3.2
Abelian differentials of the third kind

In this subsection we are interested in the Laurent expansions for the Abelian differentials of the third kind
\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}:=\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{j}}W(P,Q)
,
j=1,\dots,m
, near the poles
\infty^{0},\dots,\infty^{m}
of the given covering
(\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}},\lambda)
. We start with the following explicit formula for the differential
\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)
involving the Weierstrass zeta function (
2.20
).

Proposition 3.2

For
j=1,\dots,m
, the normalized Abelian differential of the third kind
\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)
is given by

\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)=\bigg(2\zeta(1/2)(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})+\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{j})-\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{0})\bigg)\phi(P),

(3.14)

where
\phi
is the normalized holomorphic differential (
3.1
). The periods of the differential
\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}
are such that

\oint_{a}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}=0\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\oint_{b}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}=2{\rm i}\pi(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}).

(3.15)

Proof:
Due to relations (
2.25
), (
2.42
) and (
3.1
) we have

\displaystyle\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)

\displaystyle:=\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{j}}W(P,Q)=\left(\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{j}}\Big(\wp(z_{P}-z_{Q})+2\zeta(1/2)\Big)dz_{Q}\right)dz_{P}

\displaystyle=\bigg(2\zeta(1/2)(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})+\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{j})-\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{0})\bigg)\phi(P).

We obtain the periods (
3.15
) using successively formula (
3.14
), equality (
2.26
) which states that
\zeta(u)=\partial_{u}\log\big(\sigma(u)\big)
, the quasi-periodicity property (
2.28
) of the Weierstrass function
\sigma
and the Legendre identity (
2.29
).

\square

The purpose of the next theorem is to calculate the quantities:

\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]:=\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P),\quad\quad\forall\ \alpha\geq 1.

(3.16)

To simplify, in what follows, we shall frequently use the following convention: for any positive integers
k_{0},\mu
, we understand the sum
\sum_{\nu=k_{0}}^{\mu}
in the
sense that

\sum_{\nu=k_{0}}^{\mu}:=\bigg(1-\sum_{j=0}^{k_{0}-1}\delta_{\mu,j}\bigg)\sum_{\nu=k_{0}}^{\mu},

(3.17)

(with
\delta
being the Kronecker delta), that is the sum
\sum_{\nu=k_{0}}^{\mu}
is 0 when
\mu<k_{0}
.

Theorem 3.1

Let
i\in\{0,1,\dots,m\}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
and
\alpha
be a positive integer. For
n\geq k\geq 1
, let
\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(i):=\mathcal{B}_{n,k}\big(x_{1}(i),\dots,x_{n-k+1}(i))
be the partial Bell polynomials (
2.1
), where the variables

\big\{x_{n}(i)\big\}_{n\geq 1}
are defined by (
3.5
).

1)

If
i\neq j
and
i\neq 0
, then the quantity (
3.16
) is as follows:

\begin{split}&\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]=2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(i)(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})\zeta(1/2)\\
&+\alpha\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\frac{1}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}(i)\Big(\zeta^{(k-1)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})-\zeta^{(k-1)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{0})\Big)\\
&=2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(i)(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})\zeta(1/2)+\alpha\mathbf{L}_{i,\alpha}^{\phi}[\mathscr{K}](\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})-\alpha\mathbf{L}_{i,\alpha}^{\phi}[\mathscr{K}](\infty^{i}-\infty^{0}),\end{split}

(3.18)

where
\mathscr{K}(u)=\log\sigma(u)
and
\mathbf{L}_{i,\alpha}^{\phi}[\mathscr{K}]
is the function defined by (
3.8
).

2)

When
j=i
, we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{j,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]&=2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})\zeta(1/2)+\alpha\mathcal{R}_{\alpha,1}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{\alpha+1}(j)\big)\\
&\quad-\alpha\sum_{\ell=4}^{\alpha}\frac{\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\ell}(j)}{\ell}G_{\ell}-\alpha\mathbf{L}_{j,\alpha}^{\phi}\big[\mathscr{K}\big](\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}),\end{split}

(3.19)

where
\mathcal{R}_{\alpha,1}
is the rational function given by (
2.11
) and (
2.13
) and
G_{\ell}
are the Eisenstein series (
2.14
).

3)

Assume that
i=0
. Then the quantity
\mathcal{I}_{0,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]
is of the following form:

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{0,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]&=2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(0)(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})\zeta(1/2)-\alpha\mathcal{R}_{\alpha,1}\big(x_{1}(0),\dots,x_{\alpha+1}(0)\big)\\
&\quad+\alpha\sum_{\ell=4}^{\alpha}\frac{\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\ell}(0)}{\ell}G_{\ell}+\alpha\mathbf{L}_{0,\alpha}^{\phi}\big[\mathscr{K}\big](\infty^{0}-\infty^{j}).\end{split}

(3.20)

Using (
3.17
), the sum
\sum_{\ell=4}^{\alpha}
in formulas (
3.19
) and (
3.20
) takes the value 0 for
\alpha<4
.

Proof:
Consider the meromorphic function
h
on the complex torus
\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
defined by

h_{0j}(P):=\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{j})-\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{0}),\quad\quad j=1,\dots,m

(3.21)

which appears in expression (
3.14
) for the differential
\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)
. The proof of the theorem relies essentially on the asymptotic behavior of the meromorphic
h_{0j}
near the point
\infty^{i}
with respect to the standard local parameter
\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)
defined by
\lambda(P)=\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)^{-n_{i}-1}
.

1)
Assume that
i\neq 0
and
i\neq j
. We observe that the function
h_{0j}
(
3.21
) is holomorphic on a small neighborhood of the point
\infty^{i}
and its Taylor series at
P=\infty^{i}
is given by

\displaystyle h_{0j}(P)

\displaystyle:=\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{j})-\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{0})

\displaystyle=\zeta\big(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j}+z_{P}-\infty^{i}\big)-\zeta\big(\infty^{i}-\infty^{0}+z_{P}-\infty^{i}\big)

\displaystyle{\underset{P\sim\infty^{i}}{=}}h_{0j}(\infty^{i})+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Big(\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})-\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{0})\Big)\frac{(z_{P}-\infty^{i})^{k}}{k!}

\displaystyle=h_{0j}(\infty^{i})+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Bigg(\frac{1}{k!}\Big[\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})-\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{0})\Big]\bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}x_{r}(i)\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)^{r}\bigg)^{k}\bigg)

\displaystyle=h_{0j}(\infty^{i})+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left\{\frac{1}{k!}\Big(\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})-\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{0})\Big)\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(i)\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)^{n}\right\},

where we used (
3.3
) in the forth equality and (
3.9
) in the last one.

This and (
3.4
) imply that the Taylor expansion for the Abelian differential (
3.14
) near the point
P=\infty^{i}
takes the following form:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P):=\Big(2\zeta(1/2)\big(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}\big)+h_{0j}(P)\Big)\phi(P)

\displaystyle\underset{P\sim\infty^{i}}{=}\bigg(2\zeta(1/2)\big(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}\big)+\zeta(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})-\zeta(\infty^{i}-\infty^{0})\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}rx_{r}(i)\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)^{r-1}\bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)

\displaystyle\quad+\Bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left\{\frac{1}{k!}\Big(\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})-\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{0})\Big)\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}rx_{r}(i)\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(i)\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)^{n+r-1}\right\}\Bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{i}(P).

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]:=\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)

\displaystyle={\alpha}x_{\alpha}(i)\bigg(2\zeta(1/2)\big(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}\big)+\zeta(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})-\zeta(\infty^{i}-\infty^{0})\bigg)

\displaystyle\quad+\big(1-\delta_{\alpha,1}\big)\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha-1}\left\{\frac{1}{k!}\Big(\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})-\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{0})\Big)\sum_{n=k}^{\alpha-1}(\alpha-n)x_{\alpha-n}(i)\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(i)\right\}

\displaystyle=2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(i)(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})\zeta(1/2)+{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(i)\Big(\zeta(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})-\zeta(\infty^{i}-\infty^{0})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\big(1-\delta_{\alpha,1}\big)\alpha\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha-1}\frac{1}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k+1}(i)\Big(\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})-\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{0})\Big)

\displaystyle=2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(i)(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})\zeta(1/2)+\alpha\sum_{k=0}^{\alpha-1}\frac{1}{(k+1)!}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k+1}(i)\Big(\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})-\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{0})\Big)

\displaystyle=2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(i)(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})\zeta(1/2)+\alpha\mathbf{L}_{i,\alpha}^{\phi}[\mathscr{K}](\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})-\alpha\mathbf{L}_{i,\alpha}^{\phi}[\mathscr{K}](\infty^{i}-\infty^{0}),

where we applied recurrence formula (
2.8
) for partial Bell polynomials in the third equality and the relation

\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,1}(i)=x_{\alpha}(i)
in the forth equality. In the last equality, we have used expressions (
3.7
) and (
3.8
) for the Bell operator
\mathbf{L}_{i,\alpha}^{\phi}
acting on the function
\mathscr{K}(u)=\log\sigma(u)
. This establishes (
3.18
).

2)
Making use of (
3.9
), (
3.10
) with
k=1
and the Laurent series (
2.33
) for the Weierstrass zeta function near 0, we see that the meromorphic function
h_{0j}
(
3.21
) behaves as follows near the point
\infty^{j}
:

\displaystyle h_{0j}(P)

\displaystyle:=\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{j})-\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{0})

\displaystyle\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\frac{1}{z_{P}-\infty^{j}}-\sum_{\ell=3}^{\infty}G_{\ell+1}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})^{\ell}-\zeta\big(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})}{k!}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})^{k}

\displaystyle=-\zeta\big(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left\{\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\big(x_{1}(j)\big)^{\ell+1}}\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}\big(x_{2}(j),\dots,x_{n-\ell+2}(j)\big)\right\}\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{n-1}

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{\ell=3}^{\infty}\sum_{n=\ell}^{\infty}G_{\ell+1}\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{n}-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left\{\frac{\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})}{k!}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{n}\right\}.

As consequence, the Laurent series of the differential
\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)
near
P=\infty^{j}
has the following form:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P):=\Big(2\big(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}\big)\zeta(1/2)+h_{0j}(P)\Big)\phi(P)

\displaystyle\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\bigg(2\big(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}\big)\zeta(1/2)-\zeta\big(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}rx_{r}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{r-1}\bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)

\displaystyle\quad+\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\left\{rx_{r}(j)\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\big(x_{1}(j)\big)^{\ell+1}}\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}\big(x_{2}(j),\dots,x_{n-\ell+2}(j)\big)\right\}\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{n+r-2}\right)d\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\sum_{\ell=3}^{\infty}\sum_{n=\ell}^{\infty}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}rx_{r}(j)G_{\ell+1}\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{n+r-1}\right)d\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)

\displaystyle\quad-\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left\{\frac{\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})}{k!}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}rx_{r}(j)\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{n+r-1}\right\}\right)d\mathbf{z}_{j}(P).

Thus we are now in position to calculate the residue
\mathcal{I}_{j,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{j,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]:=\underset{\infty^{j}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)

\displaystyle=2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})\zeta(1/2)-{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)\zeta\big(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{n=0}^{\alpha}\left\{(\alpha+1-n)x_{\alpha+1-n}(j)\sum_{\ell=0}^{n}\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\big(x_{1}(j)\big)^{\ell+1}}\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}\big(x_{2}(j),\dots,x_{n-\ell+2}(j)\big)\right\}

\displaystyle\quad-\big(1-\delta_{\alpha,1}-\delta_{\alpha,2}-\delta_{\alpha,3}\big)\sum_{\ell=3}^{\alpha-1}G_{\ell+1}\left\{\sum_{n=\ell}^{\alpha-1}(\alpha-n)x_{\alpha-n}(j)\mathcal{B}_{n,\ell}(j)\right\}

\displaystyle\quad-\big(1-\delta_{\alpha,1}\big)\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha-1}\left\{\frac{\zeta^{(k)}(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})}{k!}\sum_{n=k}^{\alpha-1}(\alpha-n)x_{\alpha-n}(j)\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(j)\right\}

\displaystyle=2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})\zeta(1/2)+\alpha\mathcal{R}_{\alpha,1}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{\alpha+1}(j)\big)

\displaystyle\quad-\big(1-\delta_{\alpha,1}-\delta_{\alpha,2}-\delta_{\alpha,3}\big)\alpha\sum_{\ell=4}^{\alpha}\frac{\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\ell}(j)}{\ell}G_{\ell}-\alpha\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\frac{\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}(j)}{k!}\zeta^{(k-1)}(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})

\displaystyle=2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)(\infty^{j}-\infty^{0})\zeta(1/2)+\alpha\mathcal{R}_{\alpha,1}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{\alpha+1}(j)\big)

\displaystyle\quad-\alpha\sum_{\ell=4}^{\alpha}\frac{\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\ell}(j)}{\ell}G_{\ell}-\alpha\mathbf{L}_{j,\alpha}^{\phi}\big[\mathscr{K}\big](\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}),

where in the third equality we used expression (
2.11
) for the rational function
\mathcal{R}_{\alpha,1}
and applied twice recurrence relation (
2.8
).

3)
We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{0,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]:=\underset{\infty^{0}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{0}+1}}\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)=-\underset{\infty^{0}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{0}+1}}\Omega_{\infty^{j}\infty^{0}}(P)=-\mathcal{I}_{0,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{j}\infty^{0}}].

Thus (
3.20
) follows from (
3.19
) by permuting the role of the indices
j
and 0.

\square

Remark 3.1

\bullet
Let
i=0,1,\dots,m
and
j=1,\dots,m
be fixed. Then the above theorem yields that the Laurent expansions of the differential
\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}
near the point
\infty^{i}
takes the following explicit form:

\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{i}}{=}\bigg(\frac{\delta_{ij}}{z_{j}(P)}-\frac{\delta_{i0}}{z_{i}(P)}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\infty}\Big(\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]\Big)\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)^{\alpha-1}\bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{i}(P),

where the coefficients
\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]
are given by formulas (
3.18
)-(
3.20
) and
\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)
is the standard local parameter near
\infty^{i}
defined by
\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)=\lambda(P)^{-\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}}
.

\bullet
Due to relations (
2.36
) and (
3.14
), the Abelian differential
\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}
can be rewritten in terms of the Jacobi
\theta_{1}
-function as follows:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\theta^{\prime}_{1}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})}{\theta_{1}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})}-\frac{\theta^{\prime}_{1}(z_{P}-\infty^{0})}{\theta_{1}(z_{P}-\infty^{0})}\right)dz_{P}.

3.3
Abelian differentials of the second kind

This subsection focuses on providing an explicit expression for the differential

\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P):=\underset{\infty^{j}}{\rm res}\ \lambda(Q)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}}W(P,Q),\quad\quad\quad j=0,\dots,m\quad\text{and}\quad\alpha\geq 1,

(3.22)

as well as for the coefficients of the corresponding Laurent series near the points
\infty^{i}
,
i=0,\dots,m
. Here, as above,
W(P,Q)
denotes the genus one bidifferential given by (
2.42
) and (
3.13
).

Proposition 3.3

Assume that
j\in\{0,1,\dots,m\}
. Let
\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(j):=\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{n-k+1}(j))
,
n\geq k\geq 1
, be the partial Bell polynomials (
2.1
)-(
2.2
) and
x_{n}(j)
be the coefficients (
3.5
) appearing in Taylor’s expansion (
3.4
) of the normalized holomorphic differential
\phi
near the point
P=\infty^{j}
.
Then for any positive integer
\alpha
, the differential
\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)
(
3.22
) is given by:

\begin{split}\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)&=\bigg(2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)\zeta(1/2)-\alpha\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}(j)\wp^{(k-1)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\bigg)\phi(P)\\
&=\bigg(2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)\zeta(1/2)+\alpha\mathbf{L}_{j,\alpha}^{\phi}\big[\zeta\big](\infty^{j}-z_{P})\bigg)\phi(P).\end{split}

(3.23)

Here
\mathbf{L}_{j,\alpha}^{\phi}\big[\zeta\big]
is the function (
3.8
) obtained by the action of the Bell operator (
3.7
) on the Weierstrass zeta function.

Proof:
Using the relation
\wp(u)=-\partial_{u}\zeta(u)
(
2.25
) and expression (
3.8
) for
\mathbf{L}_{j,\alpha}^{\phi}[f]
, we deduce that the second equality in (
3.23
) is an immediate consequence of the first one.

The Taylor series of the function
x\longmapsto\wp(u-x)
at
x=0
(with
u\neq 0
) is given by

\wp(u-x){\underset{x\sim 0}{=}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{n}\frac{\wp^{(n)}(u)}{n!}x^{n}.

From this and (
3.9
), we find

\displaystyle\wp\big(z_{P}-z_{Q}\big)

\displaystyle=\wp\big((z_{P}-\infty^{j})-(z_{Q}-\infty^{j})\big)

\displaystyle{\underset{Q\sim\infty^{j}}{=}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\wp^{(k)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\Big(\mathcal{A}(Q;\infty^{j})\Big)^{k}

\displaystyle=\wp\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\wp^{(k)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\bigg(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}x_{n}(j)\big(\mathbf{z}_{j}(Q)\big)^{j}\bigg)^{k}

\displaystyle=\wp\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\wp^{(k)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\bigg(\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\mathcal{B}_{n,k}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{n-k+1}(j)\big)\big(\mathbf{z}_{j}(Q)\big)^{n}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\wp\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)+\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(j)\wp^{(k)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\big(\mathbf{z}_{j}(Q)\big)^{n}\bigg).

Accordingly, the differential
\frac{W(P,Q)}{\phi(P)}
has the following Laurent series at
Q=\infty^{j}
:

\displaystyle\frac{W(P,Q)}{\phi(P)}

\displaystyle=\Big(\wp\big(z_{P}-z_{Q}\big)+2\zeta(1/2)\Big)\phi(Q)

\displaystyle{\underset{Q\sim\infty^{j}}{=}}\Big(2\zeta(1/2)+\wp\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\Big)\bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}rx_{r}(j)\big(\mathbf{z}_{j}(Q)\big)^{r-1}\bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{j}(Q)

\displaystyle\quad+\Bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\bigg\{\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(j)\wp^{(k)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\big(\mathbf{z}_{j}(Q)\big)^{n}\bigg\}\Bigg)\bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}rx_{r}(j)\big(\mathbf{z}_{j}(Q)\big)^{r-1}\bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{j}(Q)

\displaystyle=\Big(2\zeta(1/2)+\wp\big(z_{P}-\infty^{i}\big)\Big)\bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}rx_{r}(j)\big(\mathbf{z}_{j}(Q)\big)^{r-1}\bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{j}(Q)

\displaystyle\quad+\Bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}\bigg\{\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}rx_{r}(j)\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(j)\wp^{(k)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\big(\mathbf{z}_{j}(Q)\big)^{n+r-1}\bigg\}\Bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{j}(Q),

where we used formula (
3.13
) in the first equality and Taylor’s expansion (
3.4
) for the holomorphic differential
\phi
in the second equality.

Now, the obtained behavior of the bidifferential
W(P,Q)
near
Q=\infty^{j}
and recurrence formula (
2.8
) for the partial exponential Bell polynomials imply that

\displaystyle\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P):=\underset{\infty^{j}}{\rm res}\ \lambda(Q)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}}W(P,Q)

\displaystyle=\Big(2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)\zeta(1/2)+{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)\wp(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\Big)\phi(P)

\displaystyle+\left\{\big(1-\delta_{\alpha,1}\big)\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha-1}\sum_{n=k}^{\alpha-1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}(\alpha-n)x_{\alpha-n}(j)\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(j)\wp^{(k)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\right\}\phi(P)

\displaystyle=\Big(2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)\zeta(1/2)+{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)\wp(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\Big)\phi(P)

\displaystyle\quad+\left\{\big(1-\delta_{\alpha,1}\big)\alpha\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha-1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(k+1)!}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k+1}(j)\wp^{(k)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\right\}\phi(P).

Finally, since
\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,1}(j)=x_{\alpha}(j)
by (
2.3
), we get the expression claimed in the proposition.

\square

In the next proposition we study the characteristic properties of the differential
\Psi_{j,\alpha}
(
3.23
).

Proposition 3.4

Let
j=0,\dots,m
,
\alpha
be a positive integer and
\Psi_{j,\alpha}
be the differential defined by (
3.23
). Then
\Psi_{j,\alpha}
is a normalized Abelian differential of the
second kind. It has the only pole at the point
\infty^{j}
with the following principal part:

\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{\alpha+1}}+\mathcal{O}(1)\Big)d\mathbf{z}_{j}(P),\quad\quad\quad\lambda(P)=\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{-n_{j}-1}.

(3.24)

Moreover, the periods of the differential
\Psi_{j,\alpha}
are as follows:

\oint_{a}\Psi_{j,\alpha}=0\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\oint_{b}\Psi_{j,\alpha}=2{\rm i}\pi{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j).

(3.25)

Proof:
This statement follows from definition (
3.22
) of
\Psi_{j,\alpha}
together with the properties (
2.43
)-(
2.44
) of the bidifferential
W(P,Q)
(
2.42
) (see
[
40
,
35
]
for details), but let us give a direct proof using expression (
3.23
).

Due to (
3.23
), the periodicity (resp. the quasi-periodicity) property of the function
\wp
(resp.
\zeta
) and Lendgre’s identity (
2.29
) and the fact that
\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,1}(j)=x_{\alpha}(j)
, we have

\displaystyle\oint_{a}\Psi_{j,\alpha}=2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)\zeta(1/2)+\alpha\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,1}(j)\int_{x}^{x+1}\wp(z_{P}-\infty^{j})dz_{P}=0;

\displaystyle\oint_{b}\Psi_{j,\alpha}=2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)\tau\zeta(1/2)+\alpha\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,1}(j)\int_{x}^{x+\tau}\wp(z_{P}-\infty^{i})dz_{P}=2{\rm i}\pi{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j).

Now, in order to arrive at the asymptotic behavior (
3.24
), we shall calculate the residue of the differentials
(z_{P}-\infty^{j})^{k}\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)
,
k=0,\dots,\alpha
, using the two local parameters
z_{P}-\infty^{j}
and
\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)=\lambda(P)^{-\frac{1}{n_{j}+1}}
near the point
P=\infty^{j}
and then compare the results.

In view of the following singular part of the function
\wp^{(k)}
near
u=0
which follows from (
2.32
)

\displaystyle\forall\ k\geq 0,\quad\quad\wp^{(k)}(u)\underset{u\sim 0}{=}(-1)^{k}\frac{(k+1)!}{u^{k+2}}+\mathcal{O}(1),

we deduce that the differential
\Psi_{j,\alpha}
(
3.23
) behaves as follows near the point
P=\infty^{j}
:

\displaystyle\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)

\displaystyle\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\frac{\alpha\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}(j)}{(z_{P}-\infty^{j})^{k+1}}+\mathcal{O}(1)\right)dz_{P}.

As consequence, we obtain

\forall\ k=0,\dots,\alpha,\quad\quad\underset{\infty^{j}}{\rm res}\Big((z_{P}-\infty^{j})^{k}\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)\Big)=\big(1-\delta_{k,0}\big)\alpha\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}(j).

(3.26)

On the other hand, with respect to the local parameter
\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)=\lambda(P)^{-\frac{1}{n_{j}+1}}
near the pole
\infty^{j}
of
\lambda
, the singular part of the differential
\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)
takes the following form:

\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\bigg(\sum_{\ell=0}^{\alpha}c_{\ell}\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{-\ell-1}+\mathcal{O}(1)\bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{j}(P).

This and Laurent expansion (
3.9
) for
\Big(\mathcal{A}(P;\infty^{j})\Big)^{k}
imply that

\displaystyle(z_{P}-\infty^{j})^{k}\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathcal{A}(P;\infty^{j})\Big)^{k}\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\bigg(\sum_{\ell=0}^{\alpha}\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}c_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{n-\ell-1}+\dots\bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)

and then

\begin{split}&\underset{\infty^{j}}{\rm res}\Big((z_{P}-\infty^{j})^{k}\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)\Big)=\sum_{\ell=k}^{\alpha}c_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\ell,k}(j)\\
&=\delta_{k,0}c_{0}+\big(1-\delta_{k,0}\big)\sum_{\ell=k}^{\alpha}c_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\ell,k}(j),\end{split}\quad\quad\quad\quad\forall\ k=0,\dots,\alpha.

(3.27)

Finally, the results in (
3.26
) and (
3.27
) prove that
c_{0}=0
and

\forall\ k=1,\dots,\alpha,\quad\quad\sum_{\ell=k}^{\alpha}c_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\ell,k}(j)=\alpha\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}(j)

and this leads to the following system:

\displaystyle c_{\alpha}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\alpha}=\alpha\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\alpha};

\displaystyle c_{\alpha-1}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1,\alpha-1}+c_{\alpha}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\alpha-1}=\alpha\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\alpha-1};

\displaystyle\quad\quad\vdots

\displaystyle c_{1}\mathcal{B}_{1,1}+c_{2}\mathcal{B}_{2,1}+\dots+c_{\alpha-1}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha-1,1}+c_{\alpha}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,1}=\alpha\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,1}.

Thus by taking into account the fact that

\mathcal{B}_{n,n}(j)=\big(x_{1}(j)\big)^{n}\neq 0,\quad\quad\forall\ n,

we get

c_{\alpha}=\alpha,\quad\quad c_{\alpha-1}=\dots=c_{1}=0

as desired.

\square

Now we move on to calculate the following quantities:

\mathcal{I}_{i,\beta}[\Psi_{j,\alpha}]:=\underset{\infty^{i}}{\rm res}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{\beta}{n_{i}+1}}\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)

(3.28)

which appear as the coefficients of the Laurent expansion of the Abelian differential
\Psi_{j,\alpha}
(
3.23
) near the prescribed pole
\infty^{i}
of order
n_{i}+1
of the covering
\lambda
.

Theorem 3.2

Let
i,j\in\{0,1,\dots,m\}
and
\alpha,\beta
be two positive integers. For
n\geq k\geq 1
, denote by
\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(i):=\mathcal{B}_{n,k}(x_{1}(i),\dots,x_{n-k+1}(i))
the partial Bell polynomials
(
2.1
), with
\big\{x_{n}(i)\big\}_{n\geq 1}
being the coefficients defined by (
3.5
).

1)

If
i\neq j
, then the quantity
\mathcal{I}_{i,\beta}[\Psi_{j,\alpha}]
(
3.28
) is given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{i,\beta}[\Psi_{j,\alpha}]&=2{\alpha\beta}x_{\alpha}(j)x_{\beta}(i)\zeta(1/2)-\alpha\beta\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\sum_{\ell=1}^{\beta}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!\ell!}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}(j)\mathcal{B}_{\beta,\ell}(i)\wp^{(k+l-2)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})\\
&=2{\alpha\beta}x_{\alpha}(j)x_{\beta}(i)\zeta(1/2)+\alpha\beta\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}(j)\mathbf{L}_{i,\beta}^{\phi}\big[\mathscr{K}^{(k)}](\infty^{i}-\infty^{j}).\end{split}

(3.29)

Here, as above,
\mathbf{L}_{i,\beta}^{\phi}\big[\mathscr{K}]
denotes the function defined by (
3.8
) and
\mathscr{K}(u)=\log\sigma(u)
, with
\sigma
being the Weierstrass sigma function (
2.21
).

2)

When
i=j
, then by means of the function
\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}
(
2.11
)-(
2.13
) and the non-normalized
Eisenstein series (
2.14
), the quantity
\mathcal{I}_{j,\beta}[\Psi_{j,\alpha}]
in (
3.28
) is given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{j,\beta}[\Psi_{j,\alpha}]&=2{\alpha\beta}x_{\alpha}(j)x_{\beta}(i)\zeta(1/2)+\alpha\beta\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}(j)\mathcal{R}_{\beta+k,k+1}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{\beta+k+1}(j)\big)\\
&\quad-\alpha\beta\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\left((-1)^{k}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}(j)\sum_{\ell=1,k+\ell\geq 4}^{\beta}\frac{1}{k+\ell}\binom{k+\ell}{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\beta,\ell}(j)G_{k+\ell}\right),\end{split}

(3.30)

where, as in (
3.17
), the sum
\sum_{\ell=1,k+\ell\geq 4}^{\beta}
takes the value 0 whenever
\beta+k<4
.

Proof:
Using (
3.23
) and (
3.5
), we see that

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{i,\beta}[\Psi_{j,\alpha}]:=\underset{\infty^{i}}{\rm res}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{\beta}{n_{i}+1}}\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)

\displaystyle=2{\alpha\beta}x_{\alpha}(j)x_{\beta}(i)\zeta(1/2)-\alpha\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}(j)\underset{\infty^{i}}{\rm res}\bigg(\lambda(P)^{\frac{\beta}{n_{i}+1}}\wp^{(k-1)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\phi(P)\bigg).

Therefore, it suffices to compute the following residue:

\mathcal{I}_{i,\beta}\big[\wp^{(k)}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\phi\big]:=\underset{\infty^{i}}{\rm res}\bigg(\lambda(P)^{\frac{\beta}{n_{i}+1}}\wp^{(k)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\phi(P)\bigg),\quad\quad\forall\ k\geq 0.

(3.31)

1)
Let
k
be a nonnegative integer. According to (
3.9
), we can write the following Taylor series for the function
\wp^{(k)}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})
at
P=\infty^{i}
, with
i\neq j
:

\displaystyle\wp^{(k)}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})

\displaystyle=\wp^{(k)}(z_{P}-\infty^{i}+\infty^{i}-\infty^{j}))=\wp^{(k)}\big(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j}+\mathcal{A}(P,\infty^{i})\big)

\displaystyle\underset{P\sim\infty^{i}}{=}\wp^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{\wp^{(k+\ell)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})}{\ell!}\bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}x_{r}(i)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{r}\bigg)^{\ell}

\displaystyle=\wp^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})+\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\varepsilon=\ell}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{1}{\ell!}\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,\ell}(i)\wp^{(k+l)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})\bigg)\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)^{\varepsilon}.

From this and Taylor’s series (
3.4
) for the holomorphic differential
\phi
we find

\displaystyle\wp^{(k)}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\phi(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{i}}{=}\wp^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})\bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}rx_{r}(i)\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)^{r-1}\bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)

\displaystyle+\left\{\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\ell!}\wp^{(k+l)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})\sum_{\varepsilon=\ell}^{\infty}\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}rx_{r}(i)\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,\ell}(i)\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)^{\varepsilon+r-1}\right\}d\mathbf{z}_{i}(P).

Thus, due to recurrence relation (
2.8
) and the fact that
\mathcal{B}_{\beta,1}(i)=x_{\beta}(i)
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{i,\beta}\big[\wp^{(k)}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\phi\big]:=\underset{\infty^{i}}{\rm res}\bigg(\lambda(P)^{\frac{\beta}{n_{i}+1}}\wp^{(k)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\phi(P)\bigg)

\displaystyle={\beta}x_{\beta}(i)\wp^{(k)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})+\big(1-\delta_{\ell,1}\big)\sum_{\ell=1}^{\beta-1}\frac{1}{\ell!}\wp^{(k+l)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})\left\{\sum_{\varepsilon=\ell}^{\beta-1}(\beta-\varepsilon)x_{\beta-\varepsilon}(i)\mathcal{B}_{\varepsilon,\ell}(i)\right\}

\displaystyle=\beta\sum_{\ell=0}^{\beta-1}\frac{1}{(\ell+1)!}\mathcal{B}_{\beta,\ell+1}(i)\wp^{(k+l)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j})=\beta\sum_{\ell=1}^{\beta}\frac{1}{\ell!}\mathcal{B}_{\beta,\ell}(i)\wp^{(k+l-1)}(\infty^{i}-\infty^{j}).

The second equality in (
3.29
) is an immediate consequence of the first equality, the fact that
\wp(u)=-\mathscr{K}^{\prime\prime}(u)
and expression (
3.8
) for the action of the Bell operator on the function
\mathscr{K}(u)=\log\sigma(u)
.

2)
As above, in order to calculate the residue
\mathcal{I}_{j,\beta}\big[\wp^{(k)}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\phi\big]
(
3.31
),
we start with writing the Laurent series of the meromorphic differential
\wp^{(k)}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\phi(P)
near its pole
P=\infty^{j}
.

By (
2.32
), we know that the series expansion of
\wp^{(k)}
,
\forall\ k\geq 0
, near the origin is given by

\begin{split}\wp^{(k)}(u)&=(-1)^{k}\frac{(k+1)!}{u^{k+2}}+\sum_{\ell=2,\ell\geq k}^{\infty}(\ell+1)!G_{\ell+2}\frac{u^{\ell-k}}{(\ell-k)!}\\
