\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbf{F}_{\phi,3}&=\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+u\Big(4x_{1}x_{3}+2x_{2}^{2}\Big)+\frac{8}{3}\frac{x_{3}^{3}}{x_{1}}-8\frac{x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2}}{x_{1}^{2}}+4\frac{x_{2}^{4}x_{3}}{x_{1}^{3}}-\frac{2}{3}\frac{x_{2}^{6}}{x_{1}^{4}}\\
&\quad+\Big(8x_{1}^{2}x_{3}^{2}+8x_{1}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}+2x_{2}^{4}\Big)G_{2}+\Big(4x_{1}^{5}x_{3}-10x_{1}^{4}x_{2}^{2}\Big)G_{4}+\frac{5}{3}x_{1}^{8}G_{6}.\end{split}

(4.27)

Proof:
The WDVV solution (
4.25
) was first obtained by Dubrovin in
[
18
]
and reworked out in
[
14
,
35
]
. Here we are going to derive it again using (
4.24
). Indeed, the particular case
n=1
of (
4.24
) reads

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+\frac{5}{6}ux_{1}^{2}+\frac{1}{3}x_{1}^{4}G_{2}+\frac{1}{3}x_{1}^{3}\mathcal{R}_{2,2}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})

\displaystyle=\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+\frac{5}{6}ux_{1}^{2}+\frac{1}{3}G_{2}x_{1}^{4}+\frac{1}{3}x_{1}x_{3}.

In addition, from (
3.46
) and (
3.41
) it follows that

x_{3}=\frac{x_{1}}{2}c=\frac{x_{1}}{2}\Big(u+x_{1}^{2}G_{2}\Big)

and thus (
4.25
) holds. Note that if we consider the case
m=0
of (
4.21
), then we also arrive at (
4.25
).

Let us now deal with the case
n=2
. Applying (
4.24
) with
n=2
and using (
4.17
) and the fact that
x_{3}=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+\frac{51}{20}ux_{1}x_{2}+\frac{63}{20}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}G_{2}+\frac{9}{4}x_{1}x_{2}^{2}\mathcal{R}_{2,2}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})+\frac{3}{4}x_{2}x_{1}^{3}\mathcal{R}_{3,3}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{9}{10}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}\mathcal{R}_{3,2}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})+\frac{3}{10}x_{1}^{4}\mathcal{R}_{4,3}(x_{1},\dots,x_{5})-\frac{9}{20}x_{1}^{6}G_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+\frac{51}{20}ux_{1}x_{2}+\frac{63}{20}x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}G_{2}-\frac{3}{5}\frac{x_{2}^{4}}{x_{1}^{2}}+\frac{3}{4}x_{2}x_{4}+\frac{3}{10}x_{1}x_{5}-\frac{9}{20}x_{1}^{6}G_{4}.

Moreover, formula (
3.46
) and (
3.41
) imply that

\displaystyle x_{4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\sum_{k=1}^{2}\frac{1}{k+1}\binom{-3}{k}x_{1}^{-k}\mathcal{B}_{3,k+1}(x_{2},\dots,x_{4-k})+\sum_{k=1}^{2}\frac{1}{k}\mathcal{B}_{3,k}\mathcal{R}_{k,k}+\frac{1}{3}\mathbf{f}_{0}\mathcal{B}_{1,1}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{3}\frac{x_{2}^{3}}{x_{1}^{2}}+\frac{c}{3}x_{1}=-\frac{1}{3}\frac{x_{2}^{3}}{x_{1}^{2}}+\frac{1}{3}ux_{1}+x_{1}^{2}x_{2}G_{2}

and

\displaystyle x_{5}

\displaystyle=-x_{1}^{-1}\mathcal{B}_{4,2}(x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})+\frac{4}{3}x_{1}^{-2}\mathcal{B}_{4,3}(x_{2},x_{3})-\frac{5}{3}x_{1}^{-3}\mathcal{B}_{4,4}(x_{2})+\mathcal{B}_{3,2}\mathcal{R}_{3,2}+\frac{2}{3}cx_{2}+\frac{1}{3}x_{1}^{2}\mathbf{f}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}\frac{x_{2}^{4}}{x_{1}^{3}}+\frac{2}{3}ux_{2}+2x_{1}x_{2}^{2}G_{2}-x_{1}^{5}G_{4}.

This shows (
4.26
). We now move on to prove (
4.27
). We first calculate
x_{5},x_{6}
and
x_{7}
. By (
4.23
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{0}=c+x_{1}^{4}G_{4}=u+(4x_{1}x_{3}+2x_{2}^{2})G_{2}+x_{1}^{4}G_{4};

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{1}=-4\mathcal{B}_{4,3}G_{4}=-12x_{1}^{2}x_{2}G_{4};

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{2}=6(2x_{1}x_{3}+x_{2}^{2})G_{4}+10x_{1}^{4}G_{6}.

Moreover, since
x_{4}=0
and for all
\alpha\geq 1

\displaystyle x_{\alpha+4}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{4}\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha+2}\frac{1}{k+1}\binom{-4}{k}x_{1}^{-k}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha+3,k+1}(x_{2},\dots,x_{\alpha-k+4})+\alpha\sum_{k=2}^{3}\frac{1}{k}\mathcal{B}_{4,k}\mathcal{R}_{\alpha+k-1,k}+\frac{\alpha}{4}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\alpha-1}\frac{1}{\ell+1}\mathbf{f}_{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\ell+1},

by (
3.46
), we can deduce after some computations based on (
4.17
) that

\displaystyle x_{5}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{x_{3}^{2}}{x_{1}}-\frac{x_{2}^{2}x_{3}}{x_{1}^{2}}+\frac{1}{4}\frac{x_{2}^{4}}{x_{1}^{3}}+\frac{x_{1}}{4}u+\Big(x_{1}^{2}x_{3}+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}x_{2}^{2}\Big)G_{2}+\frac{x_{1}^{5}}{4}G_{4};

\displaystyle x_{6}

\displaystyle=-2\frac{x_{2}x_{3}^{2}}{x_{1}^{2}}+2\frac{x_{2}^{3}x_{3}}{x_{1}^{3}}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{x_{2}^{5}}{x_{1}^{4}}+\frac{x_{2}}{2}u+(2x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}+x_{2}^{3})G_{2}-\frac{5}{2}x_{1}^{4}x_{2}G_{4};

\displaystyle x_{7}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{x_{3}^{3}}{x_{1}^{2}}+3\frac{x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2}}{x_{1}^{3}}-\frac{9}{4}\frac{x_{2}^{4}x_{3}}{x_{1}^{4}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{x_{2}^{6}}{x_{1}^{5}}+\frac{3}{4}x_{3}u+\Big(3x_{1}x_{3}^{2}+\frac{3}{2}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}\Big)G_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\Big(\frac{15}{4}x_{1}^{4}x_{3}-\frac{15}{2}x_{1}^{3}x_{2}^{2}\Big)G_{4}+\frac{5}{2}x_{1}^{7}G_{6}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi,3}

\displaystyle=\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+\frac{u}{2}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{3}\frac{(4-\alpha)(8+\alpha)}{(4+\alpha)}x_{\alpha}x_{4-\alpha}+2G_{2}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{3}\sum_{\beta=1}^{3}\frac{(4-\alpha)(4-\beta)}{(4+\alpha)}x_{\alpha}x_{\beta}x_{4-\alpha}x_{4-\beta}

\displaystyle\quad+8\sum_{\alpha=1}^{3}\sum_{k=2}^{4}\frac{(4-\alpha)}{k(4+\alpha)}x_{4-\alpha}\mathcal{B}_{4,k}\mathcal{R}_{\alpha+k-1,k}

\displaystyle\quad+8\sum_{\alpha=1}^{3}\sum_{k=2,k+\alpha\geq 5}^{4}\Bigg\{\left[(-1)^{k}\frac{(n+1-\alpha)}{k(4+\alpha)}x_{4-\alpha}\mathcal{B}_{4,k}\right]\bigg[\sum_{\ell=1,k+\ell\geq 5}^{\alpha}\binom{k+\ell-1}{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\ell}\frac{G_{k+\ell-1}}{k+\ell-1}\bigg]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle=\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+u\Big(\frac{122}{35}x_{1}x_{3}+\frac{5}{3}x_{2}^{2}\Big)+\Big(\frac{208}{35}x_{1}^{2}x_{3}^{2}+\frac{592}{105}x_{1}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}+\frac{4}{3}x_{2}^{4}\Big)G_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2}{7}x_{1}x_{7}+\frac{2}{3}x_{2}x_{6}+\frac{6}{5}x_{3}x_{5}+\frac{232}{105}\frac{x_{3}^{3}}{x_{1}}-\frac{664}{105}\frac{x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2}}{x_{1}^{2}}+\frac{316}{105}\frac{x_{2}^{4}x_{3}}{x_{1}^{3}}-\frac{10}{21}\frac{x_{2}^{6}}{x_{1}^{4}}

\displaystyle\quad+\Big(\frac{92}{35}x_{1}^{5}x_{3}-\frac{130}{21}x_{1}^{4}x_{2}^{2}\Big)G_{4}+\frac{20}{21}x_{1}^{8}G_{6}

and this gives (
4.27
).

\square

Remark 4.2

(Jacobi forms for the Jacobi group of type
A_{n}
)

It is shown in
[
13
,
14
]
that the Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n)
determined by the holomorphic differential
\phi
can be identified with the Frobenius manifold structure on the orbit space of the Jacobi group
\mathcal{J}(A_{n})
of type
A_{n}
acting on the product
\mathcal{O}:=\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}^{n}\times\mathbb{H}
, with
\mathbb{H}
being the upper half-plane and
\mathbb{C}^{n}=\big\{(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n+1})\in\mathbb{C}^{n+1}:\quad\xi_{1}+\dots+\xi_{n+1}=0\big\}
. This correspondence is realized by the map (
4.28
) below and involves the so-called (weak) Jacobi forms for the Jacobi group
\mathcal{J}(A_{n})
which are holomorphic functions in
(\upsilon,\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n+1},\tau)
, invariant under permutations of
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n+1}
and have precise modular and quasi-periodicity properties
[
22
,
44
,
13
]
.
The theory of Jacobi forms was first investigated by Eichler and Zagier in
[
22
]
for the Jacobi group
\mathcal{J}(A_{1})
and generalized to the other cases of root systems by Wirthmüller in
[
44
]
and can be viewed as an analogue of polynomials invariant theory for finite Coxeter groups. In particular, there is an analogue of Chevalley’s theorem for the Jacobi group
\mathcal{J}(A_{n})

[
44
]
stating the following:

the algebra of (weak) Jacobi forms is a polynomial ring (over the graded ring
\mathbb{C}[G_{4},G_{6}]
of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
-modular forms) generated by
n+1
algebraically independent Jacobi forms
\varphi_{0},\varphi_{2},\dots,\varphi_{n+1}
of weight
0,-1,\dots,-(n+1)
, respectively. Here the weight
-k
is an integer that determines the transformation properties of the Jacobi form under modular transformations, similar to the weight of modular forms
G_{2k}
(
2.14
)-(
2.15
) (see
[
44
,
13
]
for a precise definition).

A set of generators of the algebra of Jacobi forms for the Jacobi group
\mathcal{J}(A_{n})
was obtained by Bertola in (
[
13
]
, Theorem 1.4) using the following generating function:

\begin{split}\big(\varphi_{0},\varphi_{2},\dots,\varphi_{n+1},\tau)\in\mathcal{O}/\mathcal{J}(A_{n})\longmapsto\lambda^{A_{n}}(P)&=e^{2{\rm i}\pi\upsilon}\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\frac{\theta_{1}(\mathfrak{z}_{j}-z_{P}|\tau)}{\theta_{1}(z_{P}|\tau)}\Bigg|_{\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\dots+\mathfrak{z}_{n+1}=0}\\
&=\varphi_{0}+\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(k-1)!}\varphi_{k}\wp^{(k-2)}(z_{P}|\tau),\end{split}

(4.28)

where
\mathfrak{z}_{1},\mathfrak{z}_{2},\dots,\mathfrak{z}_{n+1}
are the distinct zeros of the superpotential
\lambda^{A_{n}}
and
\upsilon=\displaystyle\oint_{a}\log\big(\lambda(P)\big)\phi(P)
and
\wp
and
\theta_{1}
are respectively the Weierstrass and Jacobi functions. The proof of the second equality in (
4.28
) is based on formula (5.102) in
[
18
]
together with the following expression for the lightest generator
\varphi_{n+1}
constructed by Wirthmüller
[
44
]
:

\varphi_{n+1}=e^{2{\rm i}\pi\upsilon}\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\frac{\theta_{1}(\mathfrak{z}_{j}|\tau)}{\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0|\tau)}\Bigg|_{\mathfrak{z}_{1}+\dots+\mathfrak{z}_{n+1}=0}.

Furthermore, in (
[
14
]
, Corollary 1.1), Bertola found explicit polynomial expressions for the Jacobi forms
\varphi_{0}
,
\varphi_{2}
,
\varphi_{n+1}
by means of flat coordinates of the metric
\eta(\phi)
(
2.49
) induced by the differential
\phi=dz_{P}
.

As an improvement of this result, the upcoming proposition provides a complete and simple description of the generators of the algebra of
\mathcal{J}(A_{n})
-Jacobi forms in terms of coordinates
\big\{u,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},\tau\big\}
related to
\phi
. This follows from (
4.28
) and formula (
4.22
) constructing LG superpotentials of the Hurwitz space
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n)
.

Proposition 4.8

Let
\big\{u,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},\tau\big\}
be the flat coordinates on the Frobenius manifold
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n)
associated with the holomorphic differential
dz_{P}
determined by

u:=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)dz_{P},\quad x_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\alpha}\underset{z_{P}=0}{\rm res}\lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n+1}}dz_{P},\quad\quad\tau=\oint_{b}dz_{P}.

Then the fundamental Jacobi forms
\varphi_{0},\varphi_{2},\dots,\varphi_{n+1}
with respect to the Jacobi group
\mathcal{J}(A_{n})
defined by (
4.28
) satisfy

\begin{split}&\varphi_{0}=u+\frac{n+1}{2}\mathcal{B}_{n+1,2}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})G_{2}(\tau);\\
&\varphi_{k}=\frac{(n+1)}{k}\mathcal{B}_{n+1,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n+2-k}),\quad\forall\ k=2,\dots,n+1,\end{split}

(4.29)

where
\mathcal{B}_{n+1,k}
denotes the homogeneous Bell polynomial of degree
k
(
2.1
)-(
2.2
).

In particular, the ring of
\mathcal{J}(A_{n})
-Jacobi forms is a subring of

\mathbb{C}[G_{2},G_{4},G_{6},u,x_{1},\dots,x_{n}].

\bullet

The Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n,0)
, with
n\geq 0
.

As in the preceding case, the
(n+4)
-dimensional Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n,0)
induced by the holomorphic primary differential
\phi
can be identified with the Frobenius manifold structure on the orbit space of the
\widetilde{A}_{n+1}
-extended affine Jacobi group. This was recently studied in
[
1
]
by adapting the scheme of the aforementioned
A_{n}
-Jacobi group case.

According to (
3.35
) and (
3.41
), the LG superpotentials of the Hurwitz space
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n,0)
are as follows:

\begin{split}\lambda(P)&=u+ysG_{2}(\tau)+\frac{n+1}{2}\mathcal{B}_{n+1,2}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})G_{2}(\tau)+y\Big(\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{1})-\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{0})\Big)\\
&\quad+\big(1-\delta_{n,0}\big)(n+1)\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{n+1,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n+2-k})\wp^{(k-2)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{0}\big),\end{split}

(4.30)

where
u,y,s,\tau,(x_{k})_{k\geq 1}
are the parameters related to the holomorphic differential
\phi=dz_{P}
on the torus
\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z})
and given by

\displaystyle\tau=\oint_{b}\phi,\quad\quad\quad u=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\phi(P),\quad\quad\quad s=\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{1}}\phi(P)=\infty^{1}-\infty^{0},

\displaystyle x_{k}=\frac{1}{k}\underset{\infty^{0}}{\rm res}\lambda(P)^{\frac{k}{n+1}}\phi(P),\quad\forall\ k\geq 1\quad\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\quad y=\underset{\infty^{1}}{\rm res}\lambda(P)\phi(P).

As we know from (
4.3
) and (
4.4
), the variables

\big\{u,y,s,\tau,x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\big\}

give a system of flat coordinates of the flat metric
\eta(\phi)
defined on
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n,0)
by (
2.49
).

In particular, using results from
[
1
]
and reasoning as in (
4.29
), it turns out that formula (
4.30
) allows us to obtain a basis of generators of the algebra of extended Jacobi forms for the extended Jacobi group of type
\widetilde{A}_{n+1}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}_{0}=u+ysG_{2}(\tau)+\frac{n+1}{2}\mathcal{B}_{n+1,2}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})G_{2}(\tau);

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}_{1}=-y;

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}_{k}=\frac{(n+1)}{k}\mathcal{B}_{n+1,k}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n+2-k}),\quad\forall\ k=2,\dots,n+1.

Furthermore, by taking into account the fact that

y=-(n+1)x_{n+1},

we can obtain from Theorem
4.1
the following explicit form of WDVV solution associated with the Frobenius manifold structure on the orbit space of the
\widetilde{A}_{n+1}
-extended affine Jacobi group.

Proposition 4.9

Let
n
be a nonnegative integer and
\big\{u,s,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y,\tau\big\}
be the variables defined above. Then the
\phi
-WDVV solution corresponding to the Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n,0)
is the function
\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,n}=\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,n}(u,s,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y,\tau)
determined by

\begin{split}&\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,n}=\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+uys+\frac{u}{2}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{(n+1-\alpha)(2n+2+\alpha)}{(n+1+\alpha)}x_{\alpha}x_{n+1-\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}(ys)^{2}G_{2}(\tau)\\
&+\frac{n+1}{2}ysG_{2}(\tau)\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\left(\frac{n+1-\alpha}{n+1+\alpha}+\frac{n+1-\alpha}{n+1}\right)x_{\alpha}x_{n+1-\alpha}\\
&+\frac{n+1}{2}G_{2}(\tau)\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\sum_{\beta=1}^{n}\frac{(n+1-\alpha)(n+1-\beta)}{(n+1+\alpha)}x_{\alpha}x_{\beta}x_{n+1-\alpha}x_{n+1-\beta}\\
&-y^{2}\mathscr{K}(s|\tau)+\frac{n+1}{2}y\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}\frac{1}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{n+1,k}\mathscr{K}^{(k-1)}(-s|\tau)+\frac{n+1}{2}y\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\frac{(n+1-\alpha)}{(n+1+\alpha)}x_{n+1-\alpha}\mathbf{L}_{\alpha}^{\phi}\big[\mathscr{K}(\cdot|\tau)\big](-s)\\
&-\frac{n+1}{2}y\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\left(\frac{n+1-\alpha}{n+1+\alpha}+\frac{n+1-\alpha}{n+1}\right)x_{n+1-\alpha}\mathcal{R}_{\alpha,1}+\frac{(n+1)^{2}}{2}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}\frac{(n+1-\alpha)}{k(n+1+\alpha)}x_{n+1-\alpha}\mathcal{B}_{n+1,k}\mathcal{R}_{\alpha+k-1,k}\\
&+\frac{(n+1)^{2}}{2}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n}\sum_{k=1,k+\alpha\geq 5}^{n+1}\Bigg\{\left[(-1)^{k}\frac{(n+1-\alpha)}{k(n+1+\alpha)}x_{n+1-\alpha}\mathcal{B}_{n+1,k}\right]\bigg[\sum_{\ell=1,k+\ell\geq 5}^{\alpha}\binom{k+\ell-1}{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\ell}\frac{G_{k+\ell-1}(\tau)}{k+\ell-1}\bigg]\Bigg\}\\
&+\frac{n+1}{2}y\sum_{\ell=4}^{n}\frac{1}{\ell(\ell+1)}\mathcal{B}_{n+1,\ell+1}G_{\ell}(\tau)+\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\log(x_{1}y)+\frac{\log(-1)}{2}y^{2}-\frac{3}{4}\frac{n+2}{n+1}y^{2}.\end{split}

(4.31)

Here, as in Theorem
4.1
,
\mathscr{K}:=\log\sigma
,
\mathbf{L}_{\alpha}^{\phi}\big[\mathscr{K}\big]:=\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\frac{1}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}\mathscr{K}^{(k)}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mu,\nu}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\alpha+k-1,k}
are the functions of the variables
(x_{k})_{k}
defined by (
2.1
) and (
2.13
), respectively.

Using expressions (
4.24
) and (
4.31
) for the
\phi
-WDVV solutions corresponding to the Hurwitz-Frobenius manifolds
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n)
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n,0)
, we deduce that they are linked by the following limiting behavior.

Corollary 4.2

Let
n
be a positive integer. With the notation of Propositions
4.7
and
4.9
, the WDVV solutions
\mathbf{F}_{\phi,n}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,n}
given respectively by (
4.24
) and (
4.31
) satisfy

\lim_{y\to 0}\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,n}(u,s,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y,\tau)=\mathbf{F}_{\phi,n}(u,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},\tau).

(4.32)

Note that when the considered Hurwitz space is of type
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n,0)
, formulas (
3.44
) and (
3.46
) read respectively as

\displaystyle\mathbf{f}_{\ell}

\displaystyle:=\delta_{\ell,0}\Big(u+ysG_{2}+\frac{n+1}{2}\mathcal{B}_{n+1,2}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\big)G_{2}\Big)-\frac{(-1)^{\ell}}{\ell!}y\mathscr{K}^{(\ell+1)}(s)

\displaystyle\quad+(n+1)\sum_{k=1,k+\ell\geq 4}^{n+1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k+\ell}\binom{k+\ell}{\ell}\mathcal{B}_{n+1,k}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{n+2-k}\big)G_{k+\ell}

and

\displaystyle x_{n+1+\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{n+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n+\alpha-1}\frac{1}{k+1}\binom{-n-1}{k}x_{1}^{-k}\mathcal{B}_{n+\alpha,k+1}\big(x_{2},\dots,x_{n+\alpha-k+1}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k}\mathcal{B}_{n+1,k}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{n+2-k}\big)\mathcal{R}_{\alpha+k-1,k}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{\alpha+k}\big)+\frac{\alpha}{n+1}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\alpha-1}\frac{\mathbf{f}_{\ell}}{\ell+1}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,\ell+1}\big(x_{1},\dots,x_{\alpha-\ell}\big).

By utilizing this and applying formula (
4.31
) while adopting the scheme of the proof of Corollary
4.1
,
we derive, after carrying out some simplification, the following examples of
\phi
-WDVV solutions associated with the low-dimensional Frobenius manifolds
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n,0)
,
n=0,1,2,3
.

Corollary 4.3

We have the following special cases
n=0,1,2,3
of the WDVV solution (
4.31
):

\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,0}=\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+uys+y^{2}\log(y)+\frac{1}{2}(ys)^{2}G_{2}(\tau)-y^{2}\mathscr{K}(s|\tau)+\big(\log(-1)-3/2\big)y^{2};

(4.33)

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,1}&=\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+u(ys+x^{2})+\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\log(xy)+\frac{1}{2}\big(y^{2}s^{2}+2x^{2}ys+x^{4}\big)G_{2}(\tau)-y^{2}\mathscr{K}(s|\tau)\\
&\quad-x^{2}y\mathscr{K}^{\prime}(s|\tau)+\frac{\log(-1)}{2}y^{2}-\frac{7}{4}y^{2};\end{split}

(4.34)

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,2}&=\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+u(ys+3x_{1}x_{2})+2\frac{x_{2}^{2}y}{x_{1}}-\frac{3}{4}\frac{x_{2}^{4}}{x_{1}^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\log(x_{1}y)+\frac{1}{2}\Big(y^{2}s^{2}+6x_{1}x_{2}ys+9x_{1}^{2}x_{2}^{2}\Big)G_{2}(\tau)\\
&\quad-\frac{3}{4}x_{1}^{6}G_{4}-y^{2}\mathscr{K}(s|\tau)-3x_{1}x_{2}y\mathscr{K}^{\prime}(s|\tau)+\frac{1}{2}x_{1}^{3}y\mathscr{K}^{\prime\prime}(s|\tau)+\frac{\log(-1)}{2}y^{2}-\frac{3}{4}y^{2};\end{split}

(4.35)

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,3}&=\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+uys+4ux_{1}x_{3}+2ux_{2}^{2}+2\frac{x_{2}x_{3}y}{x_{1}}+\frac{2}{3}\frac{x_{2}^{3}y}{x_{1}^{2}}+\frac{8}{3}\frac{x_{3}^{3}}{x_{1}}-8\frac{x_{2}^{2}x_{3}^{2}}{x_{1}^{2}}+8\frac{x_{2}^{4}x_{3}}{x_{1}^{3}}-\frac{2}{3}\frac{x_{2}^{6}}{x_{1}^{4}}\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2}y^{2}\log(x_{1}y)+\Big(\frac{1}{2}y^{2}s^{2}+4x_{1}x_{3}ys+2x_{2}^{2}ys+8x_{1}^{2}x_{3}^{2}+8x_{1}x_{2}^{2}x_{3}+2x_{2}^{4}\Big)G_{2}(\tau)\\
&\quad+\Big(4x_{1}^{5}x_{3}-10x_{1}^{4}x_{2}^{2}\Big)G_{4}(\tau)+\frac{5}{3}x_{1}^{8}G_{6}(\tau)-y^{2}\mathscr{K}(s|\tau)-\Big(4x_{1}x_{3}y+2x_{2}^{2}y\Big)\mathscr{K}^{\prime}(s|\tau)\\
&\quad+2x_{1}^{2}x_{2}y\mathscr{K}^{\prime\prime}(s|\tau)-\frac{1}{6}x_{1}^{4}y\mathscr{K}^{\prime\prime\prime}(s|\tau)+\frac{\log(-1)}{2}y^{2}-\frac{3}{4}y^{2}.\end{split}

(4.36)

4.3
Applications

According to formula (
2.58
), all the prepotentials of the Hurwitz-Frobenius structures induced by Dubrovin’s primary differentials (
2.46
) share the same Hessian matrix. We shall employ this geometric viewpoint to determine the derivatives of the Weierstrass functions with respect to the modulus
\tau
. Our proposed formulas take on a very concise form using the introduced function
\mathscr{W}
in (
4.39
) and its derivatives. The obtained results can be found in
[
7
]
(Section 10) and the references therein.

Proposition 4.10

Let
\sigma
be the Weierstrass function (
2.21
),
\mathscr{K}(v):=\log\sigma(v)
and
G_{2}
be the quasi-modular Eisenstein series (
2.16
).
Then we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\partial_{\tau}\Big(\mathscr{K}(v|\tau)-\frac{v^{2}}{2}G_{2}(\tau)\Big)=-\frac{1}{4{\rm{i}}\pi}\mathscr{W}(v);\end{split}

(4.37)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\partial_{\tau}\Big(\mathscr{K}^{\prime}(v|\tau)-vG_{2}(\tau)\Big)=-\frac{1}{4{\rm{i}}\pi}\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(v),\end{split}

(4.38)

where
\mathscr{W}
is defined by

\mathscr{W}(v)=\mathscr{W}(v|\tau):=\wp(v)-\Big(\zeta(v)-G_{2}(\tau)v\Big)^{2}-2G_{2}(\tau)

(4.39)

and
\wp
and
\zeta
are the Weierstrass functions (
2.19
)-(
2.20
).

The function
\mathscr{W}
is even and multivalued. Indeed, because of the quasi-periodicity property of the function
\zeta
and Legendre’s identity (
2.29
), the function
\mathscr{W}
(
4.39
) satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}(v+1)-\mathscr{W}(v)=0;

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}(v+\tau)-\mathscr{W}(v)=4{\rm i}\pi\Big(\zeta(v)-G_{2}(\tau)v\Big)-(2{\rm i}\pi)^{2}.

Furthermore, making use of the differential equation
\wp^{\prime\prime}=6\wp^{2}-30G_{4}
and observing that

\displaystyle 2\big(\zeta-G_{2}v\big)\mathscr{W}^{\prime}=4\wp^{2}-2\wp^{\prime}\big(\zeta-G_{2}v\big)-4\big(\wp+G_{2}\big)\mathscr{W}-4G_{2}\wp-8G_{2}^{2},

then we can see that the function
\mathscr{W}
(
4.39
) obeys the following differential equation

\mathscr{W}^{\prime\prime}=2\big(\zeta-G_{2}v\big)\mathscr{W}^{\prime}+4\big(\wp+G_{2}\big)\mathscr{W}-6(5G_{4}-G_{2}^{2}).

(4.40)

To prove the preceding proposition, we place ourselves in the context of the four dimensional Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold
\mathcal{H}_{1}(0,0)
whose prepotential and LG superpotential are respectively given by (
4.33
) and the case
n=0
of (
4.30
), i.e.

\begin{split}&\lambda(P)=u+ysG_{2}(\tau)+y\Big(\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{1})-\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{0})\Big),\quad\quad\text{with}\\
&u=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)dz_{P},\quad\quad y=\underset{\infty^{1}}{\rm res}\ \lambda(P)dz_{P}\quad\text{and}\quad s=\infty^{1}-\infty^{0}.\end{split}

(4.41)

From (
2.46
), we have four primary differentials given by

\phi(P)=dz_{P},\quad\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{1}},\quad\underset{\infty^{1}}{\rm res}\lambda(Q)W(P,Q)=\Big(\wp(z_{P}-\infty^{1})+G_{2}(\tau)\Big)dz_{P},\quad\quad\oint_{a}\lambda(Q)W(P,Q),

where
W(P,Q)
is the bidifferential (
2.42
) on the torus
\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z})
. The last primary differential is described by the following useful lemma.

Lemma 4.5

Let
W(P,Q)
be the genus one bidifferential (
2.42
),
\lambda
be the meromorphic function (
4.41
) and
\mathscr{W}
be the multivalued function (
4.39
). Then

\phi_{\mathbf{u}}(P):=\oint_{a}\lambda(Q)W(P,Q)=-\frac{y}{2}\Big(\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(z_{P}-\infty^{1})-\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(z_{P}-\infty^{0})\Big)dz_{P}.

(4.42)

Proof:
Putting
\alpha_{0}=-y
and
\alpha_{1}=y
, we observe that the elliptic function (
4.41
) takes the following form

\lambda(P)=u+ysG_{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{j}).

Therefore we can write

\displaystyle\oint_{a}\lambda(Q)\frac{W(P,Q)}{dz_{P}}=\oint_{a}\lambda(Q)\Big(\wp(z_{P}-z_{Q})+G_{2}\Big)dz_{Q}

\displaystyle=G_{2}\oint_{a}\lambda(Q)dz_{Q}+\big(u+ysG_{2}\big)\oint_{a}\wp(z_{P}-z_{Q})dz_{Q}+\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\oint_{a}\zeta(z_{Q}-\infty^{j})\wp(z_{Q}-z_{P})dz_{Q}

\displaystyle=-ysG_{2}^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\int_{x-z_{P}}^{x-z_{P}+1}\zeta(v+z_{P}-\infty^{j})\wp(v)dv

\displaystyle=-ysG_{2}^{2}+\bigg(\int_{x-z_{P}}^{x-z_{P}+1}\wp(v)dv\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\bigg)+\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\int_{x-z_{P}}^{x-z_{P}+1}\bigg(\frac{\wp^{\prime}(v)-\wp^{\prime}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})}{2\big(\wp(v)-\wp(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\big)}\bigg)\wp(v)dv

\displaystyle=-G_{2}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\Big(\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{j})-G_{2}(\tau)(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\int_{x-z_{P}}^{x-z_{P}+1}\bigg(\frac{\wp^{\prime}(v)-\wp^{\prime}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})}{2\big(\wp(v)-\wp(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\big)}\bigg)\bigg(\wp(v)-\wp(z_{P}-\infty^{j})+\wp(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\bigg)dv

\displaystyle=-G_{2}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\Big(\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{j})-G_{2}(\tau)(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\Big)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\wp^{\prime}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\wp(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\int_{x-z_{P}}^{x-z_{P}+1}\frac{\wp^{\prime}(v)-\wp^{\prime}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})}{2\big(\wp(v)-\wp(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\big)}dv

\displaystyle=-G_{2}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\Big(\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{j})-G_{2}(\tau)(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\Big)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\wp^{\prime}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\wp(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\int_{x-z_{P}}^{x-z_{P}+1}\bigg(\zeta(v+z_{P}-\infty^{j})-\zeta(v)-\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\bigg)dv

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\bigg(\wp^{\prime}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})+2\Big[\wp(z_{P}-\infty^{j})+G_{2}(\tau)\Big]\Big[\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{j})-G_{2}(\tau)\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\Big]\bigg)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(z_{P}-\infty^{j}),

where we used

- expression (
2.42
) for the genus one bidifferential and the relations
G_{2}(\tau)=2\zeta(1/2)=-\oint_{a}\wp(v)dv
in the first, third and fifth equalities;

- the addition theorem (
2.31
) for the
\zeta
-function in the fourth and seventh equalities;

- the fact that
ys=y(\infty^{1}-\infty^{0})=-\sum_{j=0}^{1}\alpha_{j}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})
in the fifth equality;

- the relation
\zeta(v)=\partial_{v}\log\big(\sigma(v)\big)
(
2.26
) and the quasi-periodicity properties (
2.28
) of the function
\sigma
in the eighth equality;

- expression (
4.39
) for the function
\mathscr{W}
in the last equality.

\square

Proof of Proposition
4.10
:

Although (
4.38
) follows directly from (
4.37
), both relations come from the following two special cases of formulas (
4.16
):

\begin{split}&\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{1}}(P)=2{\rm i}\pi\partial_{\tau}\partial_{y}\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,0}(u,y,s,\tau);\\
&\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\bigg(\underset{\infty^{1}}{{\rm res}\ }\lambda(Q)W(P,Q)\bigg)=2{\rm i}\pi\partial_{\tau}\partial_{s}\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,0}(u,y,s,\tau),\end{split}

(4.43)

where
\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,0}
is the WDVV solution (
4.33
). In other words, we have two ways to calculate the integrals of right hand sides of (
4.43
).

On the one hand, the function
\mathscr{W}
defined by (
4.39
) is extendable to a holomorphic function inside a fundamental parallelogram
\mathrm{F}
containing the origin with

\mathscr{W}(0):=\lim_{v\to 0}\mathscr{W}(v)=0=\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(0).

Therefore, by interchanging the order of integration and using step 1, we get

\displaystyle\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{1}}(P)=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\bigg(\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{1}}W(P,Q)\bigg)=\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{1}}\bigg(\oint_{P\in a}\lambda(P)W(P,Q)\bigg)

\displaystyle=-\frac{y}{2}\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{1}}\Big(\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(z_{Q}-\infty^{1})-\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(z_{Q}-\infty^{0})\Big)dz_{Q}=y\mathscr{W}(s)

and

\displaystyle\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\bigg(\underset{\infty^{1}}{{\rm res}\ }\lambda(Q)W(P,Q)\bigg)=\underset{\infty^{1}}{{\rm res}\ }\bigg(\lambda(Q)\oint_{P\in a}\lambda(P)W(P,Q)\bigg)

\displaystyle=-\frac{y}{2}\underset{\infty^{1}}{{\rm res}\ }\bigg(\lambda(Q)\Big(\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(z_{Q}-\infty^{1})-\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(z_{Q}-\infty^{0})\Big)dz_{Q}\bigg)=\frac{y^{2}}{2}\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(s).

On the other hand, using expression (
4.33
) for the prepotential
\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,0}(u,y,s,\tau)
, we see that

\displaystyle 2{\rm i}\pi\partial_{\tau}\partial_{y}\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,0}(u,y,s,\tau)=-{4{\rm i}\pi}y\Big(\partial_{\tau}\mathscr{K}(s)-\frac{s^{2}}{2}\partial_{\tau}G_{2}\Big);

\displaystyle 2{\rm i}\pi\partial_{\tau}\partial_{s}\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,0}(u,y,s,\tau)=-{2{\rm i}\pi}y^{2}\Big(\partial_{\tau}\mathscr{K}^{\prime}(s)-s\partial_{\tau}G_{2}(\tau)\Big).

Thus comparing these two ways using (
4.43
), we arrive at the stated relations (
4.37
) and (
4.38
).

\square

As corollary, we give a simple proof of Ramanujan’s differential equations for all the non-normalized series
G_{2m}
,
m\geq 1
.

Corollary 4.4

For any positive integer
m
, the Eisenstein series
G_{2m}
defined by (
2.14
), (
2.16
) and (
2.18
) satisfy the following Ramanujan differential equations:

\partial_{\tau}G_{2m}(\tau)=\frac{m}{2{\rm i}\pi}\bigg((2m+3)G_{2m+2}-\sum_{\ell=0}^{m-1}G_{2\ell+2}G_{2m-2\ell}\bigg).

(4.44)

Proof:
The Laurent expansions (
2.32
) and (
2.33
) for the Weierstrass functions
\wp
and
\zeta
and simple calculations enable us to obtain the following Taylor expansion for the function
\mathscr{W}
(
4.39
) near the origin:

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}(v)

\displaystyle=\wp(v)-\Big(\zeta(v)-G_{2}v\Big)^{2}-2G_{2}

\displaystyle\underset{v\sim 0}{=}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\bigg((2m+3)G_{2m+2}-\sum_{\ell=0}^{m-1}G_{2\ell+2}G_{2m-2\ell}\bigg)v^{2m}.

In particular

\displaystyle\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\bigg(2m(2m+3)G_{2m+2}-2m\sum_{\ell=0}^{m-1}G_{2\ell+2}G_{2m-2\ell}\bigg)v^{2m-1}.

This together with the equality (
4.38
) and
\tau
-derivation in the Laurent series (
2.33
) of the function
\mathscr{K}^{\prime}(v)-G_{2}v=\zeta(v)-G_{2}v
show (
4.44
).

\square

Remark 4.3

\bullet
Let
E_{2m}:=G_{2m}/2\widetilde{\zeta}(2m)
be the normalized Eisenstein series of weight
2m
, where
\widetilde{\zeta}
denotes in our case the Riemann zeta function (to distinguish it with the Weierstrass zeta function). Then making use of the following recursion formula for the Riemann zeta function that can be found in
[
43
,
4
]
):

(2m+3)\widetilde{\zeta}(2m+2)=2\sum_{k=1}^{m}\widetilde{\zeta}(2k)\widetilde{\zeta}(2m+2-2k),\quad\forall\ m\in\mathbb{N},\ m\geq 1,

(4.45)

we conclude that (
4.44
) can be expressed as follows:

\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\partial_{\tau}E_{2m}=\frac{m}{\pi^{2}\widetilde{\zeta}(2m)}\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\Big(\widetilde{\zeta}(2k+2)\widetilde{\zeta}(2m-2k)\Big)\Big(E_{2k+2}E_{2m-2k}-E_{2m+2}\Big).

This was worked out in (
[
36
]
, Theorem D) and extends the well-known Ramanujan’s differential equations for the Eisenstein series
E_{2},E_{4}
and
E_{6}

[
33
]
.

\bullet
Relations (
4.37
) and (
4.38
) and the Ramanujan differential equation (
4.44
) for the quasi-modular form
G_{2}
bring us to the following formulas for the
\tau
-derivative of Weierstrass functions
\sigma,\zeta,\wp,\wp^{(k)}
:

\begin{split}&\partial_{\tau}\log\big(\sigma(v|\tau)\big)=-\frac{1}{4{\rm{i}}\pi}\Big(\mathscr{W}(v)-\big(5G_{4}-G_{2}^{2}\big)v^{2}\Big);\\
&\partial_{\tau}\zeta(v|\tau)=-\frac{1}{4{\rm{i}}\pi}\Big(\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(v)-2\big(5G_{4}-G_{2}^{2}\big)v\Big);\\
&\partial_{\tau}\wp(v|\tau)=\frac{1}{4{\rm{i}}\pi}\Big(\mathscr{W}^{\prime\prime}(v)-2\big(5G_{4}-G_{2}^{2}\big)\Big);\\
&\partial_{\tau}\wp^{(k)}(u|\tau)=\frac{1}{4{\rm{i}}\pi}\mathscr{W}^{(k+2)}(v),\quad\quad\quad\forall\ k\geq 1.\end{split}

(4.46)

In addition, expression (
4.39
) for the function
\mathscr{W}
and differential equation (
4.40
) as well as the above relations lead to the following heat equation

\partial_{\tau}\mathscr{W}(v|\tau)=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\Big(\mathscr{W}^{\prime\prime}(v)-2\big(\wp(v)+G_{2}\big)\mathscr{W}\Big).

This turns out to obtain the second order
\tau
-derivative of Weierstrass functions by means of the function
\mathscr{W}
(
4.39
) and its derivatives.

Proposition 4.11

Consider the function
\psi
defined by

\psi(v):=\log\sigma(v)-\frac{G_{2}}{2}v^{2}=\log\bigg(\frac{\theta_{1}(v)}{\theta^{\prime}_{1}(0)}\bigg),

(4.47)

where
\sigma
is the Weierstrass function (
2.21
),
G_{2}
is the quasi-modular form (
2.16
) and
\theta_{1}
is the Jacobi function (
2.34
). Then the function
\psi
solves the following nonlinear partial differential equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\psi^{(4)}+6\big(\psi^{\prime\prime}\big)^{2}+12G_{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}+6\big(G_{2}^{2}-5G_{4})=0;\end{split}

(4.48)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(4{\rm{i}}\pi)\partial_{\tau}\psi=\psi^{\prime\prime}+\big(\psi^{\prime}\big)^{2}+3G_{2}.\end{split}

(4.49)

Proof:
Since

\psi^{\prime}(v)=\zeta(v)-G_{2}v\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\psi^{\prime\prime}(v)=-\wp(v)-G_{2},

it follows that (
4.48
) is an immediate consequence of the differential equation

\wp^{\prime\prime}(v)=6\wp^{2}(v)-\frac{g_{2}}{2}=6\wp^{2}(v)-30G_{4}.

Moreover, the previous expressions for
\psi^{\prime}
and
\psi^{\prime\prime}
imply that

\psi^{\prime\prime}+\big(\psi^{\prime}\big)^{2}+3G_{2}=-\mathscr{W},

(4.50)

with
\mathscr{W}
being the function (
4.39
).
Accordingly the heat equation (
4.49
) follows directly from (
4.37
) and (
4.50
).

\square

In the next theorem, we show that the 4-dimensional Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold
\mathcal{H}_{1}(0,0)
associated with the holomorphic differential
\phi=dz_{P}
is a geometric realization of partial differential equations (
4.48
)-(
4.49
) satisfied by the function
\psi
(
4.47
), in the sense that the WDVV associativity equations (
1.1
) for the corresponding prepotential
\widetilde{\mathbf{F}}_{\phi,0}
(
4.33
) are equivalent to
(
4.48
)-(
4.49
).

Theorem 4.2

Let
F=F(t_{0},t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})
be the function defined by

F=\frac{(t_{3})^{2}}{2}t_{0}+t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}+(t_{2})^{2}\log(t_{2})-(t_{2})^{2}\psi\big(t_{1}|2{\rm{i}}\pi t_{0}\big)+\quad\text{quadratic terms},

(4.51)

for some
\psi=\psi(v|\tau)
defined on an open subset of
\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{H}
, with
\mathbb{H}
being the upper half-plane.
Then the following statements are equivalent:

i)

F
satisfies the WDVV equations (
1.1
);

ii)

there is a function
f=f(\tau)
such that
\psi
is a solution to the following differential equations

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\psi^{(4)}+6\big(\psi^{\prime\prime}\big)^{2}+4f(\tau)\psi^{\prime\prime}-(4{\rm{i}}\pi)f^{\prime}(\tau)=0;\\
\\
(4{\rm{i}}\pi)\partial_{\tau}\psi=\psi^{\prime\prime}+\big(\psi^{\prime}\big)^{2}+f(\tau).\end{array}\right.

(4.52)

Proof:
Let us denote by
\mathrm{H}
the Hessian matrix of the function
F
introduced in (
4.51
). Since the matrix
\partial_{t_{3}}H
is constant and anti-diagonal, it follows that the WDVV equations (
1.1
) for the function
F
can be written as follows:

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t_{j}}\mathrm{H}\big)\big(\partial_{t_{3}}\mathrm{H}\big)\big(\partial_{t_{k}}\mathrm{H}\big)=\big(\partial_{t_{k}}\mathrm{H}\big)\big(\partial_{t_{3}}\mathrm{H}\big)\big(\partial_{t_{j}}\mathrm{H}\big),\quad\quad\forall\ j,k=0,1,2.

For all
j=0,1,2
, we see that the matrix
\partial_{t_{j}}\mathrm{H}
has the following form:

\partial_{t_{j}}\mathrm{H}=\begin{pmatrix}c_{j00}&c_{j01}&c_{j02}&0\\
c_{j10}&c_{j11}&c_{j12}&\delta_{j2}\\
c_{j20}&c_{j21}&c_{j22}&\delta_{j1}\\
0&\delta_{j2}&\delta_{j1}&\delta_{j0}\end{pmatrix},\quad\quad\text{with}\quad\quad c_{jks}:=\partial_{t_{j}}\partial_{t_{k}}\partial_{t_{s}}F.

This implies that

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t_{j}}\mathrm{H}\big)\big(\partial_{t_{3}}\mathrm{H}\big)=\begin{pmatrix}0&c_{j02}&c_{j01}&c_{j00}\\
\delta_{j2}&c_{j12}&c_{j11}&c_{j10}\\
\delta_{j1}&c_{j22}&c_{j21}&c_{j20}\\
\delta_{j0}&\delta_{j1}&\delta_{j2}&0\end{pmatrix}\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad

\displaystyle\big(\partial_{t_{3}}\mathrm{H}\big)\big(\partial_{t_{j}}\mathrm{H}\big)=\begin{pmatrix}0&\delta_{j2}&\delta_{j1}&\delta_{j0}\\
c_{j20}&c_{j21}&c_{j22}&\delta_{j1}\\
c_{j10}&c_{j11}&c_{j12}&\delta_{j2}\\
c_{j00}&c_{j01}&c_{j02}&0\end{pmatrix}.

As a consequence, we have

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\big(\partial_{t_{j}}\mathrm{H}\big)\big(\partial_{t_{3}}\mathrm{H}\big)\big(\partial_{t_{k}}\mathrm{H}\big)=\big(\partial_{t_{k}}\mathrm{H}\big)\big(\partial_{t_{3}}\mathrm{H}\big)\big(\partial_{t_{j}}\mathrm{H}\big),\quad\quad\quad\quad\forall\ j,k=0,1,2;

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad\left\{\begin{array}[]{llllllll}c_{j02}c_{k11}+c_{j01}c_{k21}+\delta_{k2}c_{j00}=\delta_{j2}c_{k00}+c_{k01}c_{j21}+c_{k02}c_{j11};\\
c_{j02}c_{k12}+c_{j01}c_{k22}+\delta_{k1}c_{j00}=\delta_{j1}c_{k00}+c_{k01}c_{j22}+c_{k02}c_{j12};\\
\delta_{j2}c_{k02}+c_{j11}c_{k22}+\delta_{k1}c_{j10}=\delta_{j1}c_{k10}+c_{k11}c_{j22}+\delta_{k2}c_{j02},\end{array}\quad\quad\forall\ j,k=0,1,2;\right.

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\quad(S)\left\{\begin{array}[]{lllllllllllllll}c_{111}c_{222}=c_{112}c_{122}+2c_{012};\\
c_{011}c_{222}=c_{112}c_{022}+c_{002};\\
c_{002}c_{122}+c_{001}c_{222}=2c_{022}c_{012};\\
c_{011}c_{122}+c_{001}=c_{111}c_{022};\\
c_{002}c_{112}+c_{001}c_{122}+c_{000}=(c_{012})^{2}+c_{011}c_{022};\\
c_{002}c_{111}+c_{001}c_{112}=2c_{012}c_{011};\\
\end{array}\right.

Now, by using (
4.51
) we can deduce that the preceding sixth equations in the system (S) are nothing but the following partial differential equations satisfied by the function
\psi
:

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllllll}&\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}+2\psi^{\prime\prime}\psi^{\prime}-2(2{\rm{i}}\pi)\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime}=0;&\quad\hbox{(E.1)}\\
&(2{\rm{i}}\pi)\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi-2\psi^{\prime\prime}\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)-\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime\prime}=0;&\quad\hbox{(E.2)}\\
&\big(\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi\big)^{\prime}-2\big(\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi\big)\psi^{\prime}+4\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime}=0;&\quad\hbox{(E.3)}\\
&(2{\rm{i}}\pi)\big(\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi\big)^{\prime}-2\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime\prime}\psi^{\prime}+2\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)=0;&\quad\hbox{(E.4)}\\
&(2{\rm{i}}\pi)\partial_{\tau}^{3}\psi-4\Big(\big(\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi\big)\psi^{\prime\prime}-\big[\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime}\big]^{2}\Big)+2\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime\prime}-2\psi^{\prime}\big(\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi\big)^{\prime}=0;&\quad\hbox{(E.5)}\\
&\big(\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi\big)\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}+\big(\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi\big)^{\prime}\psi^{\prime\prime}-2\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime}\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime\prime}=0.&\quad\hbox{(E.6)}\end{array}

Here the notation
\big(\partial_{\tau}^{r}Q\big)^{(m)}
means that
\partial_{v}^{m}\partial_{\tau}^{r}Q(v|\tau)
.
At this level, we have proved that the WDVV equations for the function
F
in (
4.51
) are equivalent to equations (E.1)-(E.6).

Let us move on to prove that
\textbf{ii)}\Rightarrow\textbf{i)}
. Differentiating with respect to the variable
v
in the second equation of the system (
4.52
) we see that (E.1) holds. Moreover, due to (
4.52
), we have

\displaystyle(4{\rm{i}}\pi)\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}+2\psi^{\prime}\psi^{\prime\prime};

\displaystyle(4{\rm{i}}\pi)\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=\psi^{(4)}+2\psi^{\prime}\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}+2\big(\psi^{\prime\prime}\big)^{2};

\displaystyle(4{\rm{i}}\pi)^{2}\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi

\displaystyle=(4{\rm{i}}\pi)\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime\prime}+2(4{\rm{i}}\pi)\psi^{\prime}\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime}+(4{\rm{i}}\pi)f^{\prime}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\psi^{(4)}+4\psi^{\prime}\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}+2\big(\psi^{\prime\prime}\big)^{2}+4\big(\psi^{\prime}\big)^{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}+(4{\rm{i}}\pi)f^{\prime}(\tau)

\displaystyle=2\psi^{(4)}+4\psi^{\prime}\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}+8\big(\psi^{\prime\prime}\big)^{2}+4\big(\psi^{\prime}\big)^{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}+4f(\tau)\psi^{\prime\prime};

\displaystyle(4{\rm{i}}\pi)^{2}\big(\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi\big)^{\prime}

\displaystyle=2\Big(\psi^{(5)}+2\psi^{\prime}\psi^{(4)}+10\psi^{\prime\prime}\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}+2\big(\psi^{\prime}\big)^{2}\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}+4\psi^{\prime}\big(\psi^{\prime\prime}\big)^{2}+2f(\tau)\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}\Big)

\displaystyle=4\Big(\psi^{\prime}\psi^{(4)}-\psi^{\prime\prime}\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}+\big(\psi^{\prime}\big)^{2}\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}+2\psi^{\prime}\big(\psi^{\prime\prime}\big)^{2}-f(\tau)\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}\Big).

From this we deduce that equations (E.2), (E.3) and (E.6) are satisfied.

Now if we differentiate with respect to
\tau
in (E.1) and with respect to
v
in (E.2) and take the sum, then we arrive at equation (E.4). Similarly, equation (E.5) comes from (E.2) and (E.3).

Conversely, assume that the function
\psi
obeys equations (E.1)-(E.6). Given that
\psi
solves (E.1), there exists a function
f(\tau)
such that

(4{\rm{i}}\pi)\partial_{\tau}\psi=\psi^{\prime\prime}+\big(\psi^{\prime}\big)^{2}+f(\tau).

In particular

\displaystyle(4{\rm{i}}\pi)^{2}\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi

\displaystyle=\big((4{\rm{i}}\pi)\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime\prime}+2\psi^{\prime}\big((4{\rm{i}}\pi)\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime}+(4{\rm{i}}\pi)f^{\prime}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\psi^{(4)}+4\psi^{\prime}\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}+2\big(\psi^{\prime\prime}\big)^{2}+4\big(\psi^{\prime}\big)^{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}+(4{\rm{i}}\pi)f^{\prime}(\tau).

On the other hand (E.2) yields that

\displaystyle(4{\rm{i}}\pi)^{2}\partial_{\tau}^{2}\psi

\displaystyle=2(4{\rm{i}}\pi)\big(\partial_{\tau}\psi\big)^{\prime\prime}+4(4{\rm{i}}\pi)\psi^{\prime\prime}\partial_{\tau}\psi

\displaystyle=2\psi^{(4)}+4\psi^{\prime}\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}+4\big(\psi^{\prime\prime}\big)^{2}+4\psi^{\prime\prime}\Big(\psi^{\prime\prime}+\big(\psi^{\prime}\big)^{2}+f(\tau)\Big)

\displaystyle=2\psi^{(4)}+4\psi^{\prime}\psi^{\prime\prime\prime}+8\big(\psi^{\prime\prime}\big)^{2}+4\big(\psi^{\prime}\big)^{2}\psi^{\prime\prime}+4f(\tau)\psi^{\prime\prime}.

Thus we must have

\psi^{(4)}+6\big(\psi^{\prime\prime}\big)^{2}+4f(\tau)\psi^{\prime\prime}-(4{\rm{i}}\pi)f^{\prime}(\tau)=0.

This establishes that
\textbf{i)}\Rightarrow\textbf{ii)}
and finishes the proof the theorem.

\square

Remark 4.4

Differential equations (
4.48
) and (
4.49
) and Ramanujan’s identity (
4.44
) for the Eisenstein series
G_{2}
enable us to conclude that the function
\psi
defined by (
4.47
) obeys differential equations (
4.52
) where we take
f(\tau)=3G_{2}(\tau)
.

5
WDVV solutions associated with the deformed Frobenius manifold structure

This section explores a generalization of Theorem
4.1
to the framework of
q
-deformed Frobenius manifold structures on the
N
-dimensional Hurwitz space
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
in genus one, with
N=2+2m+\sum_{j=0}^{m}n_{j}
and
q
being a fixed complex parameter.

Deformed Hurwitz-Frobenius manifolds were constructed in
[
41
]
and their descriptions make use of deformed bidifferentials defined on the underlying Riemann surfaces. Moreover, to calculate the corresponding WDVV prepotentials, a new method utilizing a formula similar to that in (
2.56
) was derived in
[
35
]
(Section 5). This inspires us to adapt the scheme outlined in Sections 3 and 4 as we work toward our goal.

We begin by extending the main results from Section 3 within the context of the
q
-deformation setting, where
q\in\mathbb{C}
is fixed parameter. Let us then consider a lattice
\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}
, where
\tau
is a complex number with positive imaginary part, further satisfying the requirement

1+q\tau\neq 0

(5.1)

and a meromorphic function
\lambda:\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
having a fixed ramification profile over the point at infinity
\infty\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
, where

\lambda^{-1}(\infty)=\big\{\infty^{0},\dots,\infty^{m}\in\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}\big\}
and the order of the pole
\infty^{j}
is
n_{j}+1
,
j=0,\dots,m
. Throughout this section, the meromorphic function
\lambda(P)
under consideration will be assumed to be the LG superpotential given by (
3.34
) and we will mountain the notation used there

\bullet

Normalized holomorphic
q
-differential

The genus one
q
-bidifferential on the torus
\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
is the
q
-deformation of the bidifferential
W(P,Q)
(
2.42
) and defined by

\begin{split}W_{q}(P,Q)&=W(P,Q)-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}\phi(P)\phi(Q)\\
&=\Big(\wp(z_{P}-z_{Q})+2\zeta(1/2)-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}\Big)dz_{P}dz_{Q},\end{split}\quad\quad P,Q\in\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}},

(5.2)

where
\phi(P)=dz_{P}
is the normalized holomorphic differential and
\wp,\zeta
are the Weierstrass functions. The
q
-bidifferential
W_{q}(P,Q)
reduces to the bidifferential
W(P,Q)
when
q=0
. Motivated by (
3.1
), the
q
-deformation of the usual normalized holomorphic differential is defined by means of the
q
-bidifferential
W_{q}(P,Q)
as

\phi_{q}(P):=\frac{1}{2{\rm i}\pi}\oint_{b}W_{q}(P,Q)=\frac{1}{1+q\tau}\phi(P)=\frac{dz_{P}}{1+q\tau},

(5.3)

with the second equality being an immediate consequence of expression (
5.2
) for
W_{q}(P,Q)
. The periods of the holomorphic differential
\phi_{q}
(
5.3
)
are given by

\oint_{a}\phi_{q}=\frac{1}{1+q\tau}\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\tau_{q}:=\oint_{b}\phi_{q}=\frac{\tau}{1+q\tau}

(5.4)

and then it satisfies the normalization condition:

\oint_{a}\phi_{q}+q\oint_{b}\phi_{q}=1.

(5.5)

Analogously to (
3.4
), for
j=0,1,\dots,m
, let us write the Taylor expansion of the holomorphic
q
-differential
\phi_{q}
(
5.3
) near the pole
\infty^{j}
of
\lambda
in the following form

\phi_{q}(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\bigg(\sum_{r=1}^{\infty}rx_{q,r}(j)\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{r-1}\bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{j}(P),\quad\quad\quad\quad\lambda(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}\mathbf{z}_{j}(P)^{-n_{j}-1}.

(5.6)

In particular, because of (
5.3
) and (
5.6
), the coefficients
x_{q,r}(j)
and
x_{r}(j)
in (
3.4
)-(
3.5
) are related by

x_{q,r}(j)=\frac{1}{r}\underset{\infty^{j}}{\rm res}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{r}{n_{j}+1}}\phi_{q}(P)=\frac{x_{r}(j)}{1+q\tau},\quad\quad\forall\ r\geq 1.

(5.7)

For later use and in a similar manner as in the non-deformed case (i.e. when
q=0
), we shall introduce the following objects and notation involving the coefficients
\big\{x_{q,r}(j)\}
(
5.6
)-(
5.7
) associated with the
q
-differential
\phi_{q}
:

\begin{split}&\mathcal{B}_{\mu,k}^{q}(j):=\mathcal{B}_{\mu,k}\big(x_{q,1}(j),\dots,x_{q,\mu-k+1}(j)\big);\\
&\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}^{q}(j):=\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}\big(x_{q,1}(j),\dots,x_{q,\mu+1}(j)\big);\\
&\mathbf{L}^{\phi_{q}}_{j,\alpha}:=\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\frac{1}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}^{q}(j)\partial_{v}^{k},\end{split}\quad\quad\quad j=0,1,\dots,m,

(5.8)

where
\mathcal{B}_{\mu,k}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mu,k}
are respectively partial Bell polynomial (
2.1
) and the related rational function (
2.13
) and

\mathbf{L}^{\phi_{q}}_{j,\alpha}
is the
q
-analogue of Bell operator (
3.7
).

\bullet

Abelian
q
-differentials of the third kind

For
j=1,\dots,m
, define the Abelian
q
-differential of the third kind
\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)
by

\begin{split}\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)&:=\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{j}}W_{q}(P,Q)=\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}s^{j}\phi(P)\\
&=\bigg(\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{j})-\zeta(z_{P}-\infty^{0})+2\zeta(1/2)s^{j}-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}s^{j}\bigg)\phi(P),\end{split}

(5.9)

where
W_{q}(P,Q)
is the
q
-bidifferential (
5.2
),
\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}
is the normalized differential of the third kind (
3.14
) and
s^{j}=\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{j}}\phi=\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}
. A straightforward calculation using (
3.15
) and the first line in (
5.9
) shows that the Abelian
q
-differential of the third kind
\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)
is normalized by the property

\oint_{a}\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)+q\oint_{b}\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)=0.

Let
i=0,\dots,m
. Then, formula (
5.9
) implies that the Laurent expansion of the
q
-differential
\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)
near the point
\infty^{i}
takes the following form

\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{i}}{=}\bigg(\frac{\delta_{ij}}{z_{i}(P)}-\frac{\delta_{i0}}{z_{i}(P)}+\sum_{\alpha=1}^{\infty}\Big(\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]\Big)\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)^{\alpha-1}\bigg)d\mathbf{z}_{i}(P),\quad\quad\lambda(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{i}}{=}\mathbf{z}_{i}(P)^{-n_{i}-1},

where the coefficients
\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]
are the
q
-analogue of
\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]
(
3.16
) and given by

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]&:=\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}}\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)\\
&=\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(i)s^{j},\end{split}\quad\quad\forall\ \alpha\geq 1

(5.10)

Therefore, expressions (
3.18
)-(
3.20
) for the terms
\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]
and formula (
5.10
) show that the coefficients
\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]
are explicitly describable in terms of the variables
\tau
,
s^{j}=\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}
and
x_{r}(j)
(
3.5
) associated with the usual normalized holomorphic differential
\phi(P)=dz_{P}
.

As another consequence of (
5.9
), we mention that

\begin{split}\mathbf{s}^{k}\big(\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}\big)&:=p.v.\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{k}}\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}(P)\\
&=\mathbf{s}^{k}\big(\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}\big)-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}s^{j}s^{k},\end{split}\quad\quad\quad\forall\ k,j=1,\dots,m,

(5.11)

where, as above, the principal value is defined by subtracting the divergent part of the integral as a function of the local parameters
\mathbf{z}_{0}(P):=\lambda(P)^{-\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}}
and
\mathbf{z}_{j}(P):=\lambda(P)^{-\frac{1}{n_{j}+1}}
near the points
\infty^{0}
and
\infty^{j}
, respectively and
\mathbf{s}^{k}\big(\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}\big)=\mathbf{s}^{k}\big(\phi_{\mathbf{s}^{j}}\big)
is explicitly determined by (
4.7
)-(
4.8
).

\bullet

Abelian
q
-differentials of the second kind

By following (
3.22
), for fixed
j=0,\dots,m
and fixed positive integer
\alpha
, consider the meromorphic
q
-differential

\Psi_{q,j,\alpha}(P):=\underset{\infty^{j}}{\rm res}\ \lambda(Q)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}}W_{q}(P,Q)=\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}\alpha x_{\alpha}(j)\phi(P),

(5.12)

where
W_{q}(P,Q)
denotes the
q
-bidifferential (
5.2
),
\Psi_{j,\alpha}
is the Abelian differential of the second kind (
3.22
) and as above
\alpha x_{\alpha}(j)
are defined by Taylor’s series (
3.4
) for the differential
\phi
near the pole
\infty^{j}
of
\lambda
. Formula (
5.12
) implies that the meromorphic
q
-differential
\Psi_{q,j,\alpha}(P)
has the same singularity structure (
3.24
) as the Abelian differential
\Psi_{j,\alpha}(P)
. In addition, expression (
3.23
) for
\Psi_{j,\alpha}
turns out to the following explicit for its
q
-analogue

\Psi_{q,j,\alpha}(P)=\bigg(2{\alpha}x_{\alpha}(j)\zeta(1/2)-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}\alpha x_{\alpha}(j)-\alpha\sum_{k=1}^{\alpha}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{\alpha,k}(j)\wp^{(k-1)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\big)\bigg)\phi(P).

On the other hand, as in (
5.10
), the following holds true for all
i=0,\dots,m
and all
\beta\geq 1
:

\begin{split}\mathcal{I}_{i,\beta}[\Psi_{q,j,\alpha}]&:=\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{\beta}{n_{i}+1}}\Psi_{q,j,\alpha}(P)\\
&=\mathcal{I}_{i,\beta}[\Psi_{j,\alpha}]-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}{\alpha\beta}x_{\alpha}(j)x_{\beta}(i).\end{split}

(5.13)

\bullet

Primary q-differentials and flat coordinates of the corresponding flat metrics

In the deformation setting depending on the fixed parameter
q\in\mathbb{C}
, we also have
N
semi-simple Frobenius manifold structures on the
N
-dimensional Hurwitz space
\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
in genus one, with
N=2+2m+\sum_{j=0}^{m}n_{j}
. These structures are associated with the following list of
N
primary
q
-differentials defined on a surface
\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}
, with
\Im\tau>0
and
1+q\tau\neq 0
(see
[
41
,
35
]
for details):

\begin{split}\phi_{q,\mathbf{t}^{i,\alpha}}(P)&:=\frac{\sqrt{n_{i}+1}}{\alpha}\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(Q)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{i}+1}}W_{q}(P,Q)=\frac{\sqrt{n_{i}+1}}{\alpha}\Psi_{q,i,\alpha}(P),\quad\begin{array}[]{ll}&i=0,\dots,m,\\
&\alpha=1,\dots,n_{i};\end{array}\\
\phi_{q,\mathbf{v}^{i}}(P)&:=\underset{\infty^{i}}{{\rm res}\ }\lambda(Q)W_{q}(P,Q)=\Psi_{q,i,n_{i}+1}(P),\quad\quad\quad i=1,\dots,m;\\
\phi_{q,\mathbf{s}^{i}}(P)&:=\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{i}}W_{q}(P,Q)=\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P),\quad\quad\quad\quad i=1,\dots,m;\\
\phi_{q,\mathbf{r}}(P)&:=\frac{1}{2{\rm{i}}\pi}\oint_{b}W_{q}(P,Q)=\phi_{q}(P);\\
\phi_{q,\xi}(P)&:=\oint_{a}\lambda(Q)W_{q}(P,Q)+q\oint_{b}\lambda(Q)W_{q}(P,Q)\\
&=\phi_{\mathbf{u}}(P)+q\phi_{\widehat{\mathbf{u}}}(P)-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}\big(u+q\widehat{u}\big)\phi(P),\end{split}

(5.14)

where
\lambda(P)
is the meromorphic function given by (
3.3
) and satisfying the conditions indicated in Remark
3.2
,
\Psi_{q,j,\mu}(P)
is the Abelian
q
-differential of the second kind (
5.12
),
\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{i}}(P)
is the Abelian
q
-differential of the third kind (
5.9
),
\phi_{q}(P)
is the holomorphic
q
-differential (
5.3
),
\phi_{\mathbf{u}}(P)
is the multivalued differential in (
2.46
) and

\phi_{\widehat{\mathbf{u}}}(P):=\oint_{b}\lambda(P)W(P,Q),\quad\quad u=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)dz_{P},\quad\quad\widehat{u}=\oint_{b}\lambda(P)dz_{P}.

Apart from the multivalued
q
-differential
\phi_{q,\xi}
, the
q
-differential (
5.14
) have the same appearance of Dubrovin’s primary differentials (
2.46
), but with respect to the
q
-bidifferential
W_{q}(P,Q)
(
5.2
). In addition, from
[
35
]
, all the
N
primary
q
-differentials in (
5.14
) are also quasi-homogeneous and their degrees of quasi-homogeneity occur in a manner similar to (
2.47
)-(
2.48
) and represent the power of
\lambda(Q)
in their integral representation formulas relative to the
q
-bidifferential
W_{q}(P,Q)
.

Now, let us assume that
\omega_{q,0}
is one of previously mentioned primary
q
-differentials (
5.14
) of quasi-homogeneous degree
d_{0}\geq 0
. Then from
[
41
]
and as in (
2.49
), we know that the formula

\eta(\omega_{q,0}):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\big(\omega_{q,0}(P_{j})\big)^{2}(d\lambda_{j})^{2}

(5.15)

defines a Darboux-Egoroff metric (which is the metric of the Frobenius manifold) on the open set
\mathcal{M}_{\omega_{q,0}}
of equivalence classes
[(\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}),\lambda)]\in\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
such that

1+q\tau\neq 0\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\omega_{q,0}(P_{j}):=\frac{\omega_{q,0}(P)}{d\mathbf{x}_{j}(P)}\Big|_{P=P_{j}}\neq 0,\quad\quad\forall\ j,

where
P_{1},\dots,P_{N}
are the simple ramification points of the covering
\big(\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}),\lambda\big)
and
\mathbf{x}_{j}(P)=\sqrt{\lambda(P)-\lambda_{j}}
is the standard local parameter near
P_{j}
. Note that, since the genus one
q
-bidifferential
W_{q}(P,Q)
(
5.2
) turns out to satisfy variational formulas which look like those for the bidifferential (
2.45
), the rotation coefficients
\beta_{ij}(q)
of the metric
\eta(\omega_{q,0})
are of the form:

\beta_{ij}(q)=\frac{1}{2}W_{q}(P_{i},P_{j}):=\frac{1}{2}\frac{W_{q}(P,Q)}{d\mathbf{x}_{i}(P)d\mathbf{x}_{j}(Q)}\bigg|_{P=P_{i},Q=P_{j}},\quad\quad i\neq j.

In addition, the following set is a system of flat coordinates (satisfying analogous duality relations as (
2.54
)) for the metric
\eta(\omega_{q,0})
:

\Big\{\mathbf{t}^{i,\alpha}(\omega_{q,0}),\mathbf{v}^{i}(\omega_{q,0}),\mathbf{s}^{i}(\omega_{q,0}),\mathbf{r}(\omega_{q,0}),\xi(\omega_{q,0})\Big\},

where
\mathbf{t}^{i,\alpha}
,
\mathbf{v}^{i}
,
\mathbf{s}^{i}
,
\mathbf{r}
are the same operations defined by (
2.51
) and
\xi
is the following one

\xi(\omega_{q,0}):=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\omega_{q,0}(P)+q\oint_{b}\lambda(P)\omega_{q,0}(P)

(5.16)

which plays the role of the last flat coordinate
\mathbf{u}(\omega_{0})
in (
2.51
) and in particular, the coordinates
\xi(\omega_{q,0})
and
\mathbf{r}(\omega_{q,0})
are dual to each other with respect to the metric
\eta(\omega_{q,0})
, where by this we mean (as above) that

\eta(\omega_{q,0})\big(\partial_{\mathbf{t}^{A}(\omega_{q,0})},\partial_{\xi(\omega_{q,0})}\big)=\delta_{\mathbf{t}^{A},\mathbf{r}},\quad\quad\forall\ \mathbf{t}^{A}.

\bullet

Prepotentials of deformed semi-simple Hurwitz-Frobenius manifolds:

According to Theorem 5.1 in
[
35
]
, the following function depending on the parameter
q\in\mathbb{C}
and looking exactly as (
2.56
) is the prepotential of the deformed Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold structure determined by the primary and quasi-homogeneous
q
-differential
\omega_{q,0}
:

\begin{split}&\mathbf{F}_{\omega_{q,0}}=\frac{1}{2(1+d_{0})}\sum_{A,B}\frac{\big((d_{0}+d_{A})\mathbf{t}^{A}(\omega_{q,0})+\rho_{\omega_{q,0},\phi_{q,\mathbf{t}^{A}}}\big)\big((d_{0}+d_{B})\mathbf{t}^{B}(\omega_{q,0})+\rho_{\omega_{q,0},\phi_{q,\mathbf{t}^{B}}}\big)}{1+d_{0}+d_{A^{\prime}}}\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}(\phi_{q,\mathbf{t}^{B^{\prime}}})\\
&\quad+\frac{\log(-1)}{4}\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{m}\mathbf{v}^{i}(\omega_{q,0})\mathbf{v}^{j}(\omega_{q,0})-\frac{3}{4(1+d_{0})}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\bigg(\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\bigg)\mathbf{v}^{i}(\omega_{q,0})\mathbf{v}^{j}(\omega_{q,0}),\end{split}

(5.17)

where
d_{0}=deg\big(\omega_{q,0}\big)
,
d_{A}=deg\big(\phi_{q,\mathbf{t}^{A}}\big)
,
d_{A^{\prime}}=deg\big(\phi_{\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}}\big)
,
\mathbf{t}^{A^{\prime}}
is the dual coordinate of
t^{A}
in according with the following relations

\displaystyle\mathbf{t}^{({i,\alpha})^{\prime}}=\mathbf{t}^{i,n_{i}+1-\alpha},\quad\quad\mathbf{v}^{{i}^{\prime}}=\mathbf{s}^{i},\quad\quad\mathbf{s}^{{i}^{\prime}}=\mathbf{v}^{i},\quad\quad\mathbf{r}^{\prime}=\xi,\quad\quad\xi^{\prime}=\mathbf{r}

and
\rho_{\omega_{q,0},\phi_{q,\mathbf{t}^{A}}}
is the
q
-analogue of the constant (
2.57
) determined by:

\rho_{\omega_{q,0},\phi_{q,\mathbf{t}^{A}}}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\delta_{\omega_{q,0},\phi_{q,\mathbf{s}^{i}}}\delta_{\phi_{q,\mathbf{t}^{A}},\phi_{q,\mathbf{s}^{j}}}\Big(\frac{1}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}\frac{1}{n_{i}+1}\Big)=\rho_{\phi_{q,\mathbf{t}^{A}},\omega_{q,0}}.

\bullet

WDVV solution associated with the normalized holomorphic
q
-differential

Let us now turn our attention to the prepotential of the Hurwitz-Frobenius manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\phi_{q}}\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
determined by the
q
-deformed holomorphic differential
\phi_{q}
(
5.3
). Here, we mention that the differential
\phi_{q}
has no zero and then the open set
\mathcal{M}_{\phi_{q}}
is only determined by condition (
5.1
). Moreover, the two flat metrics
\eta(\phi)
(
2.49
) and
\eta(\phi_{q})
(
5.15
) corresponding respectively to the usual normalized holomorphic differential
\phi(P)=dz_{P}
and to its
q
-deformation
\phi_{q}(P)=dz_{P}/(1+q\tau)
(
5.3
) are conformally related to each other, in the sense that

\eta(\phi_{q})=\frac{1}{(1+q\tau)^{2}}\eta(\phi).

(5.18)

Moreover, as a local system of flat coordinates for the flat metric
\eta(\phi_{q})
, we can take the following one which is closely linked to that (
4.3
) for the
metric
\eta(\phi)
:

\begin{split}&x_{q,\alpha}(j)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}+1}}\mathbf{t}^{j,\alpha}(\phi_{q}):=\frac{1}{\alpha}\underset{\infty^{j}}{{\rm res}}\ \lambda(P)^{\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}}\phi_{q}(P)=\frac{x_{\alpha}(j)}{1+q\tau},\quad\begin{array}[]{ll}&j=0,\dots,m,\\
&\alpha=1,\dots,n_{j};\end{array}\\
&x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)=(n_{j}+1)\mathbf{v}^{j}(\phi)=\underset{\infty^{j}}{{\rm res}\ }\lambda(P)\phi_{q}(P)=\frac{x_{n_{j}+1}(j)}{1+q\tau},\hskip 51.21504ptj=1,\dots,m;\\
&s_{q}^{j}:=\mathbf{s}^{j}(\phi_{q}):=\int_{\infty^{0}}^{\infty^{i}}\phi_{q}(P)=\frac{\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}}{1+q\tau}=\frac{s^{j}}{1+q\tau},\hskip 79.6678ptj=1,\dots,m;\\
&\tau_{q}:={2{\rm{i}}\pi}\mathbf{r}(\phi_{q}):=\oint_{b}\phi_{q}(P)=\frac{\tau}{1+q\tau};\\
&\xi_{q}:=\xi(\phi_{q}):=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\phi_{q}(P)+q\oint_{b}\lambda(P)\phi_{q}(P).\end{split}

(5.19)

The latter coordinate
\xi_{q}
is described in the upcoming lemma as a function of flat coordinates (
4.3
) related to the holomorphic differential
\phi(P)=dz_{P}
.

Lemma 5.1

Let
\big(\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}),\lambda\big)
be the covering from Theorem
3.3
, with
\Im\tau>0
and
1+q\tau\neq 0
,
\phi
and
\phi_{q}
be respectively the usual normalized holomorphic differential (
3.1
) and its
q
-deformation (
5.3
).
Then, with the notation of Theorem
3.3
, we have

\begin{split}\xi_{q}&:=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\phi_{q}(P)+q\oint_{b}\lambda(P)\phi_{q}(P)\\
&=u+\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{n_{j}+1}(j)s^{j}+\frac{{{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+1,2}(j),\end{split}

(5.20)

where
\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+1,2}(j):=\big(1-\delta_{n_{j},0}\big)\sum_{\beta=1}^{n_{j}}(n_{j}+1)x_{\beta}(j)x_{n_{j}+1-\beta}(j)
,
s^{j}=\infty^{j}-\infty^{0}
and
u:=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\phi(P)
.
Conversely, the
a
-period
u
of the Abelian differential
\lambda(P)\phi(P)
can be expressed in terms of flat coordinates (
5.19
) as follows:

\begin{split}&u:=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\phi(P)=\xi_{q}-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}-\frac{{{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+1,2}^{q}(j),\end{split}

(5.21)

with
\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+1,2}^{q}(j):=\big(1-\delta_{n_{j},0}\big)\sum_{\beta=1}^{n_{j}}x_{q,\beta}(j)x_{q,n_{j}+1-\beta}(j)
.

Proof:
Using expression (
5.3
) for the holomorphic
q
-differential
\phi_{q}
along with formulas (
3.41
) and (
3.42
) that provide the periods
u
and
\widehat{u}
of the differential
\lambda(P)\phi(P)
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\xi_{q}

\displaystyle:=\oint_{a}\lambda(P)\phi_{q}(P)+q\oint_{b}\lambda(P)\phi_{q}(P)=\frac{u+q\widehat{u}}{1+q\tau}

\displaystyle=c-2\Big(\zeta(1/2)-{{\rm i}\pi}\frac{q}{1+q\tau}\Big)\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{n_{j}+1}(j)s^{j}-\Big(\zeta(1/2)-{{\rm i}\pi}\frac{q}{1+q\tau}\Big)\sum_{j=0}^{m})(n_{j}+1)\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+1,2}(j)

\displaystyle=u+\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{n_{j}+1}(j)s^{j}+\frac{{{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+1,2}(j),

where in the last equality we have applied (
3.41
) once again to rewrite
c
in terms of variables
\{u,\tau,s^{j},x_{\alpha}(j)\}
corresponding to the holomorphic differential
\phi(P)=dz_{P}
. On the hand, since the partial Bell polynomial
\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+1,2}
is homogeneous of degree
2
, equality (
5.21
) follows from (
5.19
) and (
5.20
).

\square

Note that, formulas (
5.19
), (
5.20
) and (
5.21
) define a change of coordinates transformation
\mathbf{T}_{q}
from
\eta(\phi)
-flat coordinates (
4.3
) to their deformations (
5.19
), where
\mathbf{T}_{q}
is given by

\begin{split}&\mathbf{T}_{q}\Big(\tau,x_{1}(0),\dots,x_{n_{0}}(0),x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{n_{j}+1}(j),s^{j},u\Big)\\
&=\frac{1}{1+q\tau}\bigg(\tau,x_{1}(0),\ \dots,x_{n_{0}}(0),x_{1}(j),\ \dots,x_{n_{j}+1}(j),s^{j},(1+q\tau)u\bigg)\\
&\quad+\frac{{{\rm i}\pi}q}{1+q\tau}\bigg(0,\ \dots\,0,2\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{n_{j}+1}(j)s^{j}+\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+1,2}(j)\bigg)\\
&=\Big(\tau_{q},x_{q,1}(0),\dots,x_{q,n_{0}}(0),x_{q,1}(j),\dots,x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j),s_{q}^{j},\xi_{q}\Big)\end{split}

(5.22)

and its inverse is such that
\mathbf{T}_{q}^{-1}=\mathbf{T}_{-q}
.

Our main result here states that the WDVV solution
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q}}
corresponding to the holomorphic
q
-differential
\phi_{q}
can be conveniently described in terms of its non-deformed counterpart
\mathbf{F}_{\phi}
, previously obtained in Theorem
4.1
and the following homogeneous polynomial function

\begin{split}&\mathbf{Q}=\mathbf{Q}\big(\tau,x_{1}(0),\dots,x_{n_{0}}(0),x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{n_{j}+1}(j),s^{j},u\big)\\
&:=\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+u\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{n_{j}+1}(j)s^{j}+\frac{u}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\frac{(n_{i}+1-\alpha)(2n_{i}+2+\alpha)}{(n_{i}+1+\alpha)}x_{\alpha}(i)x_{n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)\end{split}

(5.23)

which is nothing but the first line in the expression for the prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\phi}
(see Theorem
4.1
).

Theorem 5.1

Consider the deformed semi-simple Frobenius manifold
\mathcal{M}_{\phi_{q}}\subset\mathcal{H}_{1}(n_{0},\dots,n_{m})
determined by

- the flat metric
\eta(\phi_{q})
induced by the holomorphic
q
-differential
\phi_{q}
(
5.3
) and given by (
5.15
) and (
5.18
);

- the system
\Big\{\tau_{q},x_{q,1}(0),\dots,x_{q,n_{0}}(0),x_{q,1}(j),\dots,x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j),s_{q}^{j},\xi_{q}\Big\}
of flat coordinates (
5.19
) of the flat metric
\eta(\phi_{q})
;

- the unit vector field
e=\partial_{\xi_{q}}
;

- the Euler vector field as the
q
-analogue of (
4.15
):

E_{q}=\xi_{q}\partial_{\xi_{q}}+\sum_{j=1}^{m}x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)\partial_{x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)}+\sum_{j=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{j}}\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}x_{q,\alpha}(j)\partial_{x_{q,\alpha}(j)}.

(5.24)

Then the WDVV solution associated with the deformed
\phi_{q}
-Frobenius manifold structure is as follows:

\begin{split}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q}}&=\frac{\xi_{q}^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau_{q}+\xi_{q}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}+\frac{\xi_{q}}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\frac{(n_{i}+1-\alpha)(2n_{i}+2+\alpha)}{(n_{i}+1+\alpha)}x_{q,\alpha}(i)x_{q,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)\\
&+(1-q\tau_{q})^{2}\mathbf{F}_{\phi}\circ\mathbf{T}_{-q}\big(\tau_{q},x_{q,1}(0),\dots,x_{q,n_{0}}(0),x_{q,1}(j),\dots,x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j),s_{q}^{j},\xi_{q}\big)\\
&-(1-q\tau)^{2}\mathbf{Q}\circ\mathbf{T}_{-q}\big(\tau_{q},x_{q,1}(0),\dots,x_{q,n_{0}}(0),x_{q,1}(j),\dots,x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j),s_{q}^{j},\xi_{q}\big)\\
&-\frac{{{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}\bigg)^{2}\\
&-\frac{{{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\frac{(n_{i}+1-\alpha)(2n_{i}+2+\alpha)}{n_{i}+1+\alpha}x_{q,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)x_{q,\alpha}(i)\bigg)\\
&-\frac{{{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\sum_{i,j=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{n_{j}}\frac{(n_{i}+1-\alpha)(n_{j}+1-\beta)}{(n_{i}+1+\alpha)}(n_{i}+1)x_{q,\alpha}(i)x_{q,\beta}(j)x_{q,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)x_{q,n_{j}+1-\beta}(j),\end{split}

(5.25)

where
\mathbf{F}_{\phi}
is the WDVV prepotential from Theorem
4.1
induced by the usual normalized holomorphic differential,
\mathbf{T}_{-q}
is the inverse of the map
\mathbf{T}_{q}
defined by (
5.22
) and
\mathbf{Q}
is the polynomial function given by (
5.23
).

The function
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q}}
is quasi-homogeneous of degree 2 with respect to Euler’s vector field (
5.24
):

E_{q}.\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q}}=2\mathbf{F}_{\phi,q}

Furthermore, the Hessian matrix of
\partial_{\xi_{q}}\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q}}
coincides the Gram constant matrix of the metric
\eta(\phi_{q})
(
5.18
) in flat coordinates (
5.19
).

Proof:
Making use of formula (
5.17
) and reasoning as in the proof of Proposition
4.1
, we arrive at the following expression for the prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q}}
which bears a resemblance to (
4.5
):

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q}}=\frac{\xi_{q}^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau_{q}+\xi_{q}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}+\frac{\xi_{q}}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\frac{(n_{i}+1-\alpha)(2n_{i}+2+\alpha)}{(n_{i}+1+\alpha)}x_{q,\alpha}(i)x_{q,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{i}+1)(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{i}+1}(i)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)\mathbf{s}^{i}\big(\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\left(\frac{n_{i}+1-\alpha}{n_{i}+1+\alpha}+\frac{n_{i}+1-\alpha}{n_{i}+1}\right)(n_{i}+1)(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha}\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i,j=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{n_{j}}\frac{(n_{i}+1-\alpha)(n_{j}+1-\beta)}{(n_{i}+1+\alpha)}(n_{i}+1)x_{q,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)x_{q,n_{j}+1-\beta}(j)\Big(\frac{1}{\alpha\beta}\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}\big[\Psi_{q,j,\beta}\big]\Big)

\displaystyle+\frac{\log(-1)}{4}\sum_{i,j=1,i\neq j}^{m}(n_{i}+1)(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{i}+1}(i)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)

\displaystyle-\frac{3}{4}\sum_{i,j=1}^{m}\bigg(\frac{(n_{i}+1)(n_{j}+1)}{n_{0}+1}+\delta_{ij}(n_{j}+1)\bigg)x_{q,n_{i}+1}(i)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j),

where
\mathbf{s}^{i}\big(\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}})
,
\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]
and
\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}\big[\Psi_{q,j,\beta}\big]
are respectively given by (
5.11
), (
5.10
) and (
5.13
).

Now, due to the change of variables (
5.19
), we easily see that formulas (
5.11
), (
5.10
) and (
5.13
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}^{i}\big(\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}\big)=\mathbf{s}^{i}\big(\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}\big)-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}s_{q}^{i}s_{q}^{j},\quad\quad\quad\quad i,j=1,\dots,m;

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{q,\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]=\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Omega_{\infty^{0}\infty^{j}}]-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}{\alpha}x_{q,\alpha}(i)s_{q}^{j},\quad\quad\quad i,j=0,1,\dots,m\quad\text{and}\quad j\neq 0;

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Psi_{q,j,\beta}]=\mathcal{I}_{i,\alpha}[\Psi_{j,\beta}]-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\alpha\beta x_{q,\alpha}(i)x_{q,\beta}(j),\quad\quad\quad i,j=0,1,\dots,m.

These relations together with the equality
x_{q,\alpha}(j)=x_{\alpha}(j)/(1+q\tau)
(
5.7
) bring us to the following expression involving the prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\phi}
(
4.5
) relative to the usual holomorphic differential
\phi
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q}}=\frac{\xi_{q}^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau_{q}+\xi_{q}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}+\frac{\xi_{q}}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\frac{(n_{i}+1-\alpha)(2n_{i}+2+\alpha)}{(n_{i}+1+\alpha)}x_{q,\alpha}(i)x_{q,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{(1+q\tau)^{2}}\Bigg\{\frac{u^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau+u\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{n_{j}+1}(j)s^{j}+\frac{u}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\frac{(n_{i}+1-\alpha)(2n_{i}+2+\alpha)}{(n_{i}+1+\alpha)}x_{\alpha}(i)x_{n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)\Bigg\}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{(1+q\tau)^{2}}\mathbf{F}_{\phi}\big(\tau,x_{1}(0),\dots,x_{n_{0}}(0),x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{n_{j}+1}(j),s^{j},u\big)

\displaystyle-\frac{{{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{i}+1)(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{i}+1}(i)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}s_{q}^{k}

\displaystyle-\frac{{{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\left(\frac{n_{i}+1-\alpha}{n_{i}+1+\alpha}+\frac{n_{i}+1-\alpha}{n_{i}+1}\right)(n_{i}+1)(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)x_{q,\alpha}(i)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}

\displaystyle-\frac{{{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\sum_{i,j=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{n_{j}}\frac{(n_{i}+1-\alpha)(n_{j}+1-\beta)}{(n_{i}+1+\alpha)}(n_{i}+1)x_{q,\alpha}(i)x_{q,\beta}(j)x_{q,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)x_{q,n_{j}+1-\beta}(j)

\displaystyle=\frac{\xi_{q}^{2}}{4{\rm i}\pi}\tau_{q}+\xi_{q}\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}+\frac{\xi_{q}}{2}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\frac{(n_{i}+1-\alpha)(2n_{i}+2+\alpha)}{(n_{i}+1+\alpha)}x_{q,\alpha}(i)x_{q,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)

\displaystyle-(1-q\tau)^{2}\mathbf{Q}\circ\mathbf{T}_{q}^{-1}\big(\tau_{q},x_{q,1}(0),\dots,x_{q,n_{0}}(0),x_{q,1}(j),\dots,x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j),s_{q}^{j},\xi_{q}\big)

\displaystyle+(1-q\tau_{q})^{2}\mathbf{F}_{\phi}\circ\mathbf{T}_{q}^{-1}\big(\tau_{q},x_{q,1}(0),\dots,x_{q,n_{0}}(0),x_{q,1}(j),\dots,x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j),s_{q}^{j},\xi_{q}\big)

\displaystyle-\frac{{{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle-\frac{{{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}\bigg)\bigg(\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\frac{(n_{i}+1-\alpha)(2n_{i}+2+\alpha)}{n_{i}+1+\alpha}x_{q,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)x_{q,\alpha}(i)\bigg)

\displaystyle-\frac{{{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\sum_{i,j=0}^{m}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{n_{i}}\sum_{\beta=1}^{n_{j}}\frac{(n_{i}+1-\alpha)(n_{j}+1-\beta)}{(n_{i}+1+\alpha)}(n_{i}+1)x_{q,\alpha}(i)x_{q,\beta}(j)x_{q,n_{i}+1-\alpha}(i)x_{q,n_{j}+1-\beta}(j).

\square

\bullet

Deformed Eisenstein series and Weierstrass functions

It would be more convenient during the rest of this section to utilize the
q
-deformed version of Eisenstein series and Weierstrass functions defined below.
In particular, formula (
5.25
) from Theorem
5.1
and these
q
-deformed special functions enable us to present the deformed WDVV solution
\mathbf{F}_{\phi_{q}}
(
5.25
) in a form closely resembling that of the prepotential
\mathbf{F}_{\phi}
from Theorem
4.1
.
This provides an efficient approach for producing the
q
-analogues of explicit WDVV solutions previously obtained in Section 4.

Let
\tau
be as in (
5.1
). For
\ell\geq 2
, define the
q
-deformation of the Eisenstein series
G_{\ell}
(
2.18
) by

G_{q,\ell}(\tau_{q}):=(1-q\tau_{q})^{-\ell}G_{\ell}\Big(\frac{\tau_{q}}{1-q\tau_{q}}\Big)-\delta_{\ell,2}\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}},\quad\quad\tau_{q}:=\frac{\tau}{1+q\tau}.

(5.26)

Note that the following
q
-analogue of Ramanujan’s differential equations (
4.44
) are satisfied:

\forall\ m\geq 1,\quad\quad\partial_{\tau_{q}}G_{q,2m}(\tau_{q})=\frac{m}{2{\rm i}\pi}\bigg((2m+3)G_{q,2m+2}(\tau_{q})-\sum_{\ell=0}^{m-1}G_{q,2\ell+2}(\tau_{q})G_{q,2m-2\ell}(\tau_{q})\bigg).

(5.27)

Let
\sigma
be the Weierstrass function (
2.21
). We shall define the
q
-deformation of the function
\mathscr{K}(v|\tau):=\log\sigma(v|\tau)
by

\mathscr{K}_{q}(v)=\mathscr{K}_{q}(v|\tau_{q}):=\mathscr{K}\Big(\frac{v}{1-q\tau_{q}}\Big|\frac{\tau_{q}}{1-q\tau_{q}}\Big)+\log(1-q\tau_{q}),\quad\quad\tau_{q}:=\frac{\tau}{1+q\tau}.

(5.28)

In particular, for any positive integer
k
we have

\mathscr{K}_{q}^{(k)}(v):=\partial_{v}^{k}\mathscr{K}_{q}(v|\tau_{q})=\frac{1}{(1-q\tau_{q})^{k}}\mathscr{K}^{(k)}\Big(\frac{v}{1-q\tau_{q}}\Big|\frac{\tau_{q}}{1-q\tau_{q}}\Big).

(5.29)

Let us point out that
q
-Eisenstein series appear in the Laurent expansion of the function
\mathscr{K}_{q}^{\prime}(v)
(which is the
q
-analogue of the Weierstrass zeta function) near
v=0
, in like manner as
(
2.33
):

\mathscr{K}_{q}^{\prime}(v)=\frac{1}{(1-q\tau_{q})}\zeta\Big(\frac{v}{1-q\tau_{q}}\Big|\tau\Big)\underset{v\sim 0}{=}\frac{1}{v}-\sum_{\ell=3}^{\infty}G_{q,\ell+1}(\tau_{q})v^{\ell}.

(5.30)

In addition, when
q
is an integer, the
b
-period
\tau_{q}=\tau/(1+q\tau)
(
5.4
) of the holomorphic
q
-differential
\phi_{q}
has also a positive imaginary part. Thus, owing to the modularity properties of Eisenstein series (
2.15
)-(
2.17
) and those of Weierstrass functions (
2.27
), formulas (
5.26
) and (
5.28
) read respectively as

G_{q,\ell}(\tau_{q})=G_{\ell}(\tau_{q})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{K}_{q}(v|\tau_{q})=\mathscr{K}(v|\tau_{q}).

The next result presents an alternative description of the LG superpotential (
3.34
) from Theorem
3.3
involving deformed Eisenstein series and Weierstrass functions (
5.26
)-(
5.29
) along with flat coordinates (
5.19
) associated with the
q
-differential
\phi_{q}
. Let us emphasize that the obtained
q
-version (
5.31
) below looks exactly as expression (
3.34
) which is nothing but the case
q=0
of (
5.31
).

Proposition 5.1

Let
\mathbb{L}
be the lattice
\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}
, with
\Im\tau>0
and
1+q\tau\neq 0
.
Let
\lambda
be a meromorphic function on the torus
\mathbb{C}/{\mathbb{L}}
given by (
3.34
).
Then, with notation (
5.26
), (
5.29
) and (
5.8
), the meromorphic function
\lambda
takes the following form:

\begin{split}\lambda(P)&=\xi_{q}+G_{q,2}(\tau_{q})\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}+\frac{1}{2}G_{q,2}(\tau_{q})\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)\mathcal{B}^{q}_{n_{j}+1,2}(j)\\
&\quad-\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n_{j}+1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+1,k}^{q}(j)\big[\partial_{v}^{k}\mathscr{K}_{q}(\cdot|\tau_{q})\big]\big((1-q\tau_{q})(z_{P}-\infty^{j})\big)\right),\end{split}

(5.31)

where
\xi_{q}
and
s_{q}^{j}
are given by (
5.19
) and (
5.20
). Moreover, for any
j=0,\dots,m
, the function
\lambda
(
5.31
) has the following Laurent expansion near its pole
\infty^{j}
of order
n_{j}+1
:

\lambda(P)\underset{P\sim\infty^{j}}{=}(n_{j}+1)\sum_{k=1}^{n_{j}+1}\frac{\mathcal{B}^{q}_{n_{j}+1,k}(j)}{k(1-q\tau_{q})^{k}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})^{k}}+\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{f}_{q,\ell}(j)(1-q\tau_{q})^{\ell}(z_{P}-\infty^{j})^{\ell},

(5.32)

where
\mathbf{f}_{q,\ell}(j)
is the quantity defined by

\begin{split}\mathbf{f}_{q,\ell}(j)&=\delta_{\ell,0}\bigg(\xi_{q}+G_{q,2}(\tau_{q})\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}+\frac{1}{2}G_{q,2}(\tau_{q})\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)\mathcal{B}^{q}_{n_{j}+1,2}(j)\bigg)\\
&\quad-\frac{1}{\ell!}\sum_{i=0,i\neq j}^{m}(n_{i}+1)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n_{i}+1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\mathcal{B}^{q}_{n_{i}+1,k}(i)\big[\partial_{v}^{k+\ell}\mathscr{K}_{q}(\cdot|\tau_{q})\big](s_{q}^{j}-s_{q}^{i})\right)\\
&\quad+(n_{j}+1)\sum_{k=1,k+\ell\geq 4}^{n_{j}+1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k+\ell}\binom{k+\ell}{\ell}\mathcal{B}^{q}_{n_{j}+1,k}(j)G_{q,k+\ell}(\tau_{q}).\end{split}

(5.33)

Here, according to convention (
3.17
), the term containing the sum
\sum_{j=1}^{m}
in formulas (
5.31
) and (
5.33
) is omitted for
m=0
. Similarly, we should understand the sum
\sum_{k=1,k+\ell\geq 4}^{n_{j}+1}
in (
5.33
).

Proof:
By (
3.34
) and (
3.41
) and the equalities
\zeta(v)=\mathscr{K}^{\prime}(v)
and
2\zeta(1/2|\tau)=G_{2}(\tau)
(
4.9
), we can write

\displaystyle\lambda(P)

\displaystyle=u+\big(1-\delta_{m,0}\big)G_{2}(\tau)\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{n_{j}+1}(j)s^{j}+\frac{1}{2}G_{2}(\tau)\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+1,2}(j)

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n_{j}+1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+1,k}\big(x_{1}(j),\dots,x_{n_{j}+2-k}(j)\big)\mathscr{K}^{(k)}\big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}|\tau\big)\right)

\displaystyle=\xi_{q}+\big(1-\delta_{m,0}\big)\bigg((1-q\tau)^{-2}G_{2}\Big(\frac{\tau_{q}}{1-q\tau_{q}}\Big)-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\bigg)\sum_{j=1}^{m}(n_{j}+1)x_{q,n_{j}+1}(j)s_{q}^{j}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\bigg((1-q\tau)^{-2}G_{2}\Big(\frac{\tau_{q}}{1-q\tau_{q}}\Big)-\frac{{2{\rm i}\pi}q}{1-q\tau_{q}}\bigg)\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)\mathcal{B}^{q}_{n_{j}+1,2}(j)

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{j=0}^{m}(n_{j}+1)\left(\sum_{k=1}^{n_{j}+1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!(1-q\tau_{q})^{k}}\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+1,k}\big(x_{q,1}(j),\dots,x_{q,n_{j}+2-k}(j)\big)\mathscr{K}^{(k)}\Big(z_{P}-\infty^{j}\Big|\frac{\tau_{q}}{1-q\tau_{q}}\Big)\right),

where, in the second equality, we used the change of coordinates
\mathbf{T}_{-q}
(
5.22
) and the homogeneity property (
2.7
) of Bell polynomials.
Thus, due to relations (
5.26
), (
5.29
) and notation (
5.8
), we conclude that (
5.31
) holds. To get the Laurent expansion (
5.32
), it suffices to use the
q
-deformation version (
5.31
) of
\lambda(P)
and the Laurent series for the derivatives of the function
\mathscr{K}_{q}(v)
and proceed as in the proof of (
3.43
).

\square

Due to the homogeneity property (
2.7
) of partial Bell polynomial and the relation
x_{q,r}(j)=(1-q\tau_{q})x_{r}(j)
from (
5.7
), we have

\mathcal{B}^{q}_{n_{j}+1,k}(j):=\mathcal{B}_{\mu,k}\big(x_{q,1}(j),\dots,x_{q,\mu-k+1}(j)\big)=(1-q\tau_{q})^{k}\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+1,k}(j).

Therefore, the principal parts in the two Laurent series (
5.32
) and (
3.43
) coincide.
Now, the uniqueness of the Laurent series expansion for the LG superpotential
\lambda(P)
implies the following link between the coefficients of their regular parts.

Corollary 5.1

Let
\lambda
be the meromorphic function on
\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z})
, with
\Im\tau>0
and
1+q\tau\neq 0
and given by formula (
3.34
) and its
q
-analogue (
5.31
). Let
j=0,\dots,m
and
\mathbf{f}_{\ell}(j)
and
\mathbf{f}_{q,\ell}(j)
be the coefficients defined respectively by (
3.44
) and (
5.33
) and appeared in the Laurent series (
3.43
) and (
5.32
) of
\lambda(P)
.
Then
\mathbf{f}_{\ell}(j)
and
\mathbf{f}_{q,\ell}(j)
are linked by

\forall\ \ell\geq 0,\quad\quad\mathbf{f}_{\ell}(j)=(1-q\tau)^{\ell}\mathbf{f}_{q,\ell}(j).

(5.34)

As another consequence, we obtain the
q
-analogue of formula (
3.46
).

Corollary 5.2

For
j=0,1,\dots,m
, let
\big\{rx_{q,r}(j)\big\}_{r}
be the coefficients of Taylor’s series (
5.6
) of the holomorphic
q
-differential
\phi_{q}
(
5.3
) near the pole
\infty^{j}
of
\lambda(P)
(
5.31
). Then for any positive integer
\alpha
, we have

\begin{split}x_{q,n_{j}+1+\alpha}(j)&=\frac{\alpha}{n_{j}+1}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{j}+\alpha-1}\frac{1}{k+1}\binom{-n_{j}-1}{k}\big(x_{q,1}(j)\big)^{-k}\mathcal{B}_{n_{j}+\alpha,k+1}\big(x_{q,2}(j),\dots,x_{q,n_{j}+\alpha-k+1}(j)\big)\\
